
[](https://lawblend.com/articles/land-acquisition-law-in-india/)



## Chapter 2: Strategic Roadmap

Before anything else, let me reframe what Chapter 2 is _for_. Looking at your full thesis architecture, Chapter 2 is the **historical-conceptual scaffold** that does three jobs: (1) it gives global comparative depth so DILRMP isn't read as a uniquely Indian phenomenon, (2) it traces the Indian genealogy that makes today's land records what they are, and (3) it sets up the political-economy critique you develop in Chapter 3 and the Odisha-specific argument you develop in Chapter 4. If Chapter 2 only does descriptive history, you've wasted it. It should _quietly_ plant the analytical seeds you'll harvest later.

A note on how to think about this chapter: your conceptual framework is in Chapter 1, so Chapter 2 should not re-do theory. Instead, it should let history _illustrate_ the concepts (legibility, dead capital, conclusive vs. presumptive titling, neoliberal governmentality) so the reader sees them at work before Chapter 3 names them again. This is the difference between a historical chapter and a _theoretically informed_ historical chapter.

---

### 1. The Reading List: Tiered by Importance

I've grouped these by how central they are to your specific chapter. Read tier 1 first and most carefully; tier 2 for breadth; tier 3 for citation cover and Odisha grounding.

#### Tier 1 — Must-read (these will appear repeatedly across your chapter)

**On the conceptual lens (use throughout, but especially §2.1 and §2.2):**

- ==**James C. Scott, _Seeing Like a State_ (1998)**== — Yale University Press. Especially the chapter on cadastral mapping and "state simplifications." This is non-negotiable. Scott's argument that cadastral maps don't merely _describe_ land tenure but _create_ it through giving categories the force of law is the single most important theoretical move you can make. Almost every critical paper on land records modernisation cites him.
- **Hernando de Soto, _The Mystery of Capital_ (2000)** — Basic Books. You need to read this _and_ its critics to set up the conclusive-titling debate. De Soto is the intellectual ancestor of DILRMP's titling logic.
- **Geoffrey Payne, Alain Durand-Lasserve & Carole Rakodi, "The Limits of Land Titling and Home Ownership," _Environment and Urbanization_ 21(2), 2009** — the cleanest empirical critique of de Soto. Pair it with the de Soto reading.

**On India specifically (§2.3 backbone):**

- **Irfan Habib, _The Agrarian System of Mughal India 1556–1707_ (revised edition, OUP)** — the canonical work on pre-colonial land records, the _zabt/dahsala_ system, and the role of the _patwari_ and _qanungo_. Even just the chapter on revenue assessment.
- **Ranajit Guha, _A Rule of Property for Bengal_ (1963/1996)** — for the intellectual origins of the Permanent Settlement and how the British constructed "property" in India. This is your most important colonial-era reading.
- **Eric Stokes, _The English Utilitarians and India_ (1959)** — for the Ryotwari and Mahalwari logic. Pair with **Burton Stein, _Thomas Munro: The Origins of the Colonial State_** for the Ryotwari biography.
- **Bernard S. Cohn, _Colonialism and Its Forms of Knowledge_ (1996)** — particularly the essay on the census and the survey. Cohn's argument that the British produced India as a knowable object through enumeration directly anchors your colonial section.

**On contemporary India and DILRMP (§2.4 and §2.5):**

- **Solomon Benjamin et al., "Bhoomi: 'E-Governance', Or, an Anti-Politics Machine Necessary to Globalize Bangalore?" (CASUMM Working Paper, 2007)** — the foundational critical study of land records digitisation in India. It found that digitisation of land records led to increased corruption and bribery and substantially increased time taken for land transactions, while facilitating very large players in the land markets to capture vast quantities of land. This paper completely overturns the World Bank "best practice" narrative and is essential. [Wordpress](https://casumm.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2008/09/bhoomi-e-governance.pdf)
- **Pritam Singh / Pritam P., "Illegibly Legible: Outcomes of a Land Records Modernisation Programme in South India," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 23(4), 2023** — the most recent rigorous academic study of land records modernisation in India. Its premise is that experiments to modernise land records have not met with predetermined standards of success due primarily to historically evolved contradictions around land as a resource and a commodity. This is exactly the kind of framing your thesis is doing — read it carefully and engage with it directly. [Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/joac.12556)
- **Centre for Policy Research (CPR), "Land Rights and the Digital Revolution in India: Potential and Pitfalls" (2022)** — the best policy-oriented overview of DILRMP, SVAMITVA, JAGA Mission, and AgriStack as an integrated stack. Useful for §2.5.
- **NCAER and IGIDR DILRMP evaluation reports** — these are the official-but-critical implementation assessments. Wikipedia notes that the implementation of the DILRMP programme across states have experienced significant shortcomings, according to reports submitted by the Indira Gandhi Institute of Development Research (IGIDR), National Institute of Public Finance and Policy (NIPFP), and National Council of Applied Economic Research (NCAER). Track these down — they give you state-level granularity. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_India_Land_Record_Modernization_Programme)

#### Tier 2 — Important supporting works

**Global / theoretical:**

- **Klaus Deininger, _Land Policies for Growth and Poverty Reduction_ (World Bank, 2003)** — the World Bank's flagship land policy doctrine document. You need this to characterise the international institutional position you are analysing critically.
- **Ben Cousins et al. on titling in South Africa** — useful comparative frame.
- **Jean-Philippe Platteau, "The Evolutionary Theory of Land Rights as Applied to Sub-Saharan Africa: A Critical Assessment," _Development and Change_ 27(1), 1996** — classic theoretical critique of the formalisation thesis.
- **Tania Murray Li, _Land's End: Capitalist Relations on an Indigenous Frontier_ (Duke, 2014)** — for understanding how titling produces dispossession even where it appears to "secure" tenure.
- **Christian Lund, "Rule and Rupture: State Formation through the Production of Property and Citizenship," _Development and Change_ 47(6), 2016** — useful for theorising the link between land records, property, and state-making.

**Indian context:**

- **D.A. Washbrook, "Law, State and Agrarian Society in Colonial India," _Modern Asian Studies_ 15(3), 1981** — a brilliant synthesis of how colonial land law shaped agrarian relations.
- **Neeraj Hatekar's work on Bombay Deccan settlements** — for the Ryotwari implementation.
- **Daniel J. Mathew, T. Haque, R. Wadhwa** and other recent NCAER and NIPFP authors on DILRMP.
- **Carnegie India / Namita Wahi's Land Rights Initiative work** — particularly _Land Acquisition in India_ and Wahi's papers on constitutional property rights post the 44th amendment.

#### Tier 3 — Odisha-specific (you'll lean on these in §2.3.3 and bridging to Chapter 4)

- **B.C. Roy, _Land Tenure System in Odisha_ (older work but indispensable)**
- **The Madhya Pradesh / Odisha Settlement Reports of S.L. Maddox** — primary source. The DDCE Utkal materials I found note that S.L. Maddox held that the early revenue settlement in Odisha was an "unfortunate record of assessment on insufficient enquiry". [DDCE Utkal University](https://ddceutkal.ac.in/Syllabus/MA_history/Paper-18.pdf)
- **World Bank Land Governance Assessment Framework: Odisha State Report (2017)** — gives you the technical baseline. It notes that Odisha State is the amalgamation of parts of British ruled Madras Presidency, Central Provinces, Bengal and 24 princely states, and inherited a colonial exploitative system of land revenue assessment. This fragmentary inheritance is crucial — Odisha is not one tenurial region but several. [World Bank](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/768771504867372908/pdf/119623-WP-P095390-PUBLIC-7-9-2017-10-7-48-Odishafinalreport.pdf)
- **Pati, Biswamoy** on Odisha agrarian history (his work on the Khurda rebellion etc.).
- **Walter Fernandes and others at NCDS Bhubaneswar** on land alienation, especially in Schedule V areas of Odisha.

### 2. How to Assemble: The Architectural Logic

This is where most students go wrong — they collect the readings but don't have a _spine_ for the chapter. Here is the spine I'd recommend, mapped to your existing section headings.

#### The argumentative spine of Chapter 2

> **Land records are not neutral administrative artefacts; they are instruments through which states make territory and people legible for purposes that change across regimes — extraction (pre-colonial and colonial), redistribution (post-independence), and now market-making and welfare-targeting (neoliberal). Each regime layers its records on top of, but does not erase, the previous one. India's contemporary land records are therefore a _palimpsest_ — and DILRMP is the latest attempt to flatten that palimpsest into a single, machine-readable surface. The friction between this flattening ambition and the layered reality is what produces the village-level outcomes I document later.**

That sentence (or your version of it) is what should run as a thread through the chapter. Every section is a step in proving it.

#### Section-by-section assembly

**§2.1 Land records and their functions** — Open by _de-naturalising_ the land record. Drawing on Scott, argue that records do four overlapping functions: (a) fiscal — taxing land; (b) juridical — defining who can sue/be sued over land; (c) economic — enabling collateralised credit and transactions (this is where de Soto enters); and (d) governmental in the Foucauldian sense — making populations and territories knowable to administer welfare, plan, surveil. Each historical regime emphasises different functions; today's DILRMP collapses all four. This typology is _your_ contribution and gives the reader a tool to use throughout the chapter.

**§2.2 Evolution globally** — Don't try to write a global history. Do _two comparative arcs_:

- **Arc 1: From feudal/customary to cadastral/freehold** (Europe, 16th–19th c.) — using Scott. The point is that the cadastre is a relatively recent invention even in Europe, and it was always coercive.
- **Arc 2: The post-WWII titling consensus** — Torrens system origins in Australia (1858), its export, the World Bank's adoption of it from the 1970s, de Soto's reformulation in the 1990s, and the contemporary "land governance" agenda. This sets up §2.2.4 and Chapter 3.

For §2.2.1 (pre-colonial elsewhere), you can be brief — a paragraph each on Ottoman _defter_, Chinese _yu lin tu_, and customary African systems — to make the point that record-keeping is universal but cadastral mapping is specifically modern-Western.

**§2.3 History in India** — This is the meatiest section. Structure it as three layers in a palimpsest:

- **§2.3.1 Pre-colonial:** Use Habib. Mughal _zabt/dahsala_ under Todar Mal from 1580 was already a sophisticated revenue-records system — _patwaris_ kept village-level records, _qanungos_ kept _pargana_-level records. The dahsala system fixed the state's annual cash demand on cultivated land by averaging recorded yields and prices of staple crops over a preceding ten-year period. Critically — this was a _revenue_ record, not a _property_ record. The distinction matters: pre-colonial records told the state what to tax, not who owned what in the modern juridical sense. This is your single most important argumentative point in this section, because it dismantles the colonial myth (still influential) that the British "introduced" land records to India. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahsala_system)
- **§2.3.2 Colonial:** Cover Permanent Settlement (1793, Bengal/Bihar/Odisha coastal), Ryotwari (1820 onwards, Madras/Bombay), Mahalwari (1822 onwards, NWP/Punjab). Don't recount the systems mechanically — extract the _records-relevant_ point. Argue, following Guha and Cohn, that the colonial settlements achieved a juridical revolution: they converted revenue obligations into property rights, and in doing so produced "the landowner" as a new legal subject. The cadastral survey (1860s onwards) was the technical infrastructure of this revolution. The records the British produced — the Record of Rights, the _khatian_, the _jamabandi_ — are still the conceptual basis of what Bhulekh shows today. Make this continuity visible.
- **§2.3.3 Post-independence:** Cover (a) zamindari abolition (1950s), (b) tenancy reform (uneven), (c) ceiling laws (largely failed), (d) the Survey & Settlement framework's _consolidation_ phase, and (e) the slow erosion of the records' currency through non-updating. Crucially, end this subsection by noting that by the 1990s the records had become so out-of-sync with reality that _something_ like DILRMP was inevitable. This sets up the genesis question for §2.5.

**§2.4 Legal and institutional infrastructure** — Keep this section _functional_, not exhaustive. The reader needs to understand: (i) land is a State subject under Schedule VII of the Constitution, hence the federal complexity; (ii) the Registration Act 1908 and the Indian Stamp Act 1899 govern _deed_ registration but not _title_; (iii) the Transfer of Property Act 1882 governs transactions; (iv) state-level Land Revenue Codes/Acts (each state different, e.g. the Odisha Land Reforms Act 1960, the Odisha Survey & Settlement Act 1958) govern records. The key analytic point: India follows a _presumptive_ (deeds-based) titling system, not a _conclusive_ (Torrens-based) one. Currently, land records systems in the country represent the Zamindari and Ryotwari models, and India at present follows the Presumptive land titling method. This is the foundational fact that makes DILRMP's titling ambition so radical — and so difficult. [ForumIAS](https://forumias.com/blog/conclusive-land-titling-system-in-india-and-its-challenges-explained-pointwise/)

**§2.5 Genesis of DILRMP** — Frame this as a brief introduction (you do the deep dive in Chapter 3). Tell the genesis story: the Computerisation of Land Records (CLR) scheme of 1988-89, the Strengthening of Revenue Administration & Updating of Land Records (SRA&ULR) of 1987-88, their merger in 2008 as NLRMP, and the 2016 rebranding as DILRMP. The Digital Land Records Modernisation Programme was initiated in 2008, originally known as the National Land Records Modernisation Programme (NLRMP), and in 2016 was restructured into a Central Sector Scheme with 100% funding from the Government of India. Note the "ultimate goal of conclusive titling" rhetorically built into NLRMP from the start. End the chapter with a one-paragraph forward look that signals Chapter 3 will analyse the political economy of this shift. [IMPRI](https://www.impriindia.com/insights/digital-land-records-dilrmp/)

**§1.7 Primer on terminology (your Chapter 1 section)** — A side note: glossary items should include _patta, pahani, khatian, jamabandi, khasra, RoR, RTC, Bhulekh, Bhunaksha, mutation, partition, ULPIN, Sabik/Hal, ekpadia, sahi_, etc. Keep definitions tight and Odisha-specific where relevant.

### 3. Plan of Action: Six-Week Schedule

Here is a realistic timeline. I'm assuming you can dedicate roughly 25–30 hours/week to this chapter. Adjust if you have teaching/fieldwork duties.

**Week 1 — Reading sprint, Tier 1 only.** Read Scott (the legibility chapter), de Soto (chapters 1–3), Habib (chapter on revenue assessment), Guha (chapters 1–4), and Benjamin et al. on Bhoomi. Take notes in a single document organised by _concept_, not by source — so you have a "legibility" file, a "conclusive titling" file, a "patwari/qanungo" file. This pays compound interest later.

**Week 2 — Outline + section drafts of §2.1, §2.2.** Write a detailed outline (one page per section, two-three sentences per sub-paragraph) and circulate it to your supervisor _before_ writing prose. This is the highest-leverage feedback you can get. Then draft §2.1 (1500-2000 words) and §2.2 (3000-4000 words). Don't worry about polish.

**Week 3 — Read Tier 2 and draft §2.3.** This is the heaviest week. Cohn, Stokes, Washbrook, Deininger, the de Soto critics. Then draft §2.3 (5000-6000 words). Aim for full coverage but rough prose.

**Week 4 — Read Tier 3 and draft §2.4 + §2.5.** Odisha-specific reading and the contemporary policy/legal literature. Draft §2.4 (2500-3000 words) and §2.5 (1500-2000 words). By end of week you should have a complete rough draft of the chapter at roughly 14000-17000 words.

**Week 5 — Restructuring and analytical sharpening.** This is where the chapter becomes a thesis chapter rather than a literature review. Re-read your full draft. For each section, ask: (a) is the argumentative spine visible? (b) am I citing primary sources where I should be? (c) does this section set up something specific in Chapters 3, 4, 5, or 6? Add cross-references forward. Cut at least 15-20% — most first drafts have padding.

**Week 6 — Polish, citations, bibliography, and supervisor submission.** Standardise citation style (Harvard or Chicago, depending on TISS norms), build the bibliography, write the chapter introduction (last, always last) and a one-paragraph chapter summary at the end. Submit to supervisor.

#### Three tactical tips that will save you weeks

First, **write the chapter introduction last**. You cannot know what you've argued until you've written it. The introduction should preview the spine, the structure, and (in one sentence) the contribution.

Second, **don't quote when you can paraphrase**. Doctoral writing rewards synthesis, not collage. A chapter that quotes Scott directly five times is weaker than one that explains Scott's argument in your own words and uses one quote for high-stakes phrasing.

Third, **build a parallel "evidence file" for Chapter 3**. As you read for Chapter 2, you'll inevitably find material more useful for Chapter 3 (political economy) or Chapter 4 (Odisha) — World Bank quotes, NITI Aayog documents, specific NCAER findings. Do not stuff them into Chapter 2 because it feels productive. Park them in a separate file. This discipline keeps Chapter 2 historically focused and gives you a head start on Chapter 3.

### A small nudge

Your thesis architecture is genuinely strong — the move from global → national → state → village → parcel is methodologically ambitious and exactly the kind of multi-scalar work that Development Studies should be doing. Chapter 2 is where you earn the right to make the political-economy argument in Chapter 3, so invest in it accordingly. The single most important thing you can do is keep asking, while writing each section: _what is the analytical reason this paragraph is here, and how does it serve the argument I'll make in Chapter 7?_ If you can answer that, the chapter will write itself.

## ==Chapter 2: Comprehensive Reference Architecture==

I've built this so that every subsection of your chapter has its own substantial reading list, plus the _argumentative move_ that the readings should support. Treat this as a working bibliography you'll prune — not every reference needs to be cited, but every one will give you something useful to think with.

I've marked entries with stars: ★★★ = read fully, ★★ = read selectively, ★ = use for citation/coverage only.

### §2.1 Land Records and Their Functions

This section establishes _what land records are_ and _what they do_ — laying the conceptual groundwork the rest of the chapter sits on. The argumentative move: land records are not neutral administrative artefacts but instruments of state power that combine fiscal, juridical, economic, and governmental functions in historically variable proportions.

#### Foundational theoretical works

- ★★★ **James C. Scott (1998)**, _Seeing Like a State: How Certain Schemes to Improve the Human Condition Have Failed_, Yale University Press. Especially the introduction and chapters 1–2 on legibility and the cadastral map. The single most important reference for this section.
- ★★★ **Bernard S. Cohn (1996)**, _Colonialism and Its Forms of Knowledge: The British in India_, Princeton University Press. Read the introduction on "investigative modalities" — survey, enumerative, observational. Survey modalities explored both natural and social landscapes allowing for the mapping of India and identification of archaeological sites, and enumerative modalities created the census infrastructure. This pairs perfectly with Scott. [Baruch Honors](https://blogs.baruch.cuny.edu/karadamodernimperialism/?p=28)
- ★★★ **Michel Foucault (1991)**, "Governmentality" in _The Foucault Effect: Studies in Governmentality_, eds. Burchell, Gordon and Miller, University of Chicago Press. For the move from sovereignty to government — useful for explaining why DILRMP is not just better tax records.
- ★★ **Christian Lund (2016)**, "Rule and Rupture: State Formation through the Production of Property and Citizenship," _Development and Change_ 47(6): 1199–1228. Argues property and citizenship are co-produced through records — directly applicable to your argument.
- ★★ **Sara Berry (1993)**, _No Condition is Permanent: The Social Dynamics of Agrarian Change in Sub-Saharan Africa_, University of Wisconsin Press. The classic argument that even where records exist, claims to land are continuously negotiated.

#### Recent work on legibility, taxation, and records

- ★★ **Mauricio Sánchez-Talanquer (2020)**, "One-Eyed State: The Politics of Legibility and Property Taxation," _Latin American Politics and Society_ 62(3): 47–75. Argues that when land ownership is disputed, landholders use inscription into state records to secure legal property rights, and to minimize tax liabilities, propertied interests may exploit opportunities to manipulate land valuations. A really useful counterpoint to Scott — landholders sometimes _want_ legibility. [Cambridge Core](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/latin-american-politics-and-society/article/abs/oneeyed-state-the-politics-of-legibility-and-property-taxation/E796B1A7F0B0C0913C6A2118646FA566)
- ★★ **Karen E. Ferree, Lauren Honig, Ellen Lust and Melanie L. Phillips (2023)**, "Land and Legibility: When Do Citizens Expect Secure Property Rights in Weak States?" _American Political Science Review_ 117(1): 42–58.
- ★★ **Lauren Honig (2022)**, "Land, Power, and Property Rights: The Political Economy of Land Titling in Sub-Saharan Africa," _American Political Science Review_. Very useful for the "demand side" of titling.
- ★ **Naím Bro (2023)**, "No taxation without informational foundation: on the role of legibility in tax state development," _Journal of Institutional Economics_.

#### Function-typology references

- ★★ **Daniel Bromley (2008)**, "Formalising Property Relations in the Developing World: The Wrong Prescription for the Wrong Malady," _Land Use Policy_ 26(1): 20–27. For the distinction between possession and recognised property.
- ★★ **Liz Alden Wily (2018)**, "Collective Land Ownership in the 21st Century: Overview of Global Trends," _Land_ 7(2): 68. Useful global comparative on how land records relate to common property.
- ★ **John Henry Merryman (1985)**, _The Civil Law Tradition_. For the deeds-vs-titles legal distinction.

---

### §2.2 Evolution of Land Records and Titling Systems (Global)

The argumentative move: cadastral mapping and individual titling are _modern_ European inventions, exported through colonialism and now repackaged by international institutions as the universal solution. This isn't natural; it's historical.

#### §2.2.1 Pre-colonial land records and practices

- ★★ **Roger J.P. Kain and Elizabeth Baigent (1992)**, _The Cadastral Map in the Service of the State: A History of Property Mapping_, University of Chicago Press. The definitive comparative history of cadastral maps. Read selectively for the European chapters.
- ★★ **Pierre Clochon and Pierre Robert (eds., various)** on French _terriers_ and _plans parcellaires_.
- ★ **Domesday Book scholarship**: No survey approaching the scope and extent of Domesday Book was attempted again in Britain until the 1873 Return of Owners of Land. The Domesday Book (1086) is your earliest "Western" example. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesday_Book)
- ★ **Stuart M. Kim (1997)**, "The Limits of Cadastral Mapping," for Korean and Chinese precedents — _yu lin tu_ maps.
- ★ **Halil İnalcık (1994)**, _An Economic and Social History of the Ottoman Empire, 1300–1914_, Cambridge University Press — for the _defter_ (Ottoman survey register) tradition.

#### §2.2.2 Colonial approaches to land records

- ★★★ **Kain & Baigent (1992)**, op cit. — chapter on French Napoleonic cadastre, the model that influenced almost all modern systems. The Napoleonic cadastre was set up by the Finance Act of 15 September 1807 as a tool of equitable taxation — it produced three documents: plans, section statuses, and matrices.
- ★★ **Andro Linklater (2013)**, _Owning the Earth: The Transforming History of Land Ownership_, Bloomsbury. Accessible global history; useful for narrative.
- ★★ **Lorenzo Cotula (ed., 2007)**, _Changes in 'Customary' Land Tenure Systems in Africa_, IIED/FAO. Some have argued that formalization risks resulting in codification and hence in loss of flexibility, which is one of the very strengths of customary systems. [International Institute for Environment and Development](https://www.iied.org/sites/default/files/pdfs/migrate/12537IIED.pdf)
- ★★ **Ben Cousins (2007)**, "More than Socially Embedded: The Distinctive Character of Communal Tenure Regimes in South Africa and its Implications for Land Policy," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 7(3): 281–315.
- ★ **Catherine Boone (2014)**, _Property and Political Order in Africa: Land Rights and the Structure of Politics_, Cambridge University Press.

#### §2.2.3 Post-colonial developments

- ★★ **Jean-Philippe Platteau (1996)**, "The Evolutionary Theory of Land Rights as Applied to Sub-Saharan Africa: A Critical Assessment," _Development and Change_ 27(1): 29–86. The classic theoretical critique of the formalisation thesis.
- ★★ **Espen Sjaastad and Daniel W. Bromley (1997)**, "Indigenous Land Rights in Sub-Saharan Africa: Appropriation, Security and Investment Demand," _World Development_ 25(4): 549–562.
- ★★ **John Bruce and Shem Migot-Adholla (eds., 1994)**, _Searching for Land Tenure Security in Africa_, World Bank. Classic statement of the early reform thinking.
- ★ **Jesse Ribot (2001)**, _Decentralization, Participation and Accountability in Sahelian Forestry_, IIED.

#### §2.2.4 Contemporary and neo-liberal approaches

- ★★★ **Hernando de Soto (2000)**, _The Mystery of Capital: Why Capitalism Triumphs in the West and Fails Everywhere Else_, Basic Books. Required.
- ★★★ **Hernando de Soto (1989)**, _The Other Path: The Invisible Revolution in the Third World_, Harper & Row. The earlier book where the argument was first made.
- ★★★ **Geoffrey Payne, Alain Durand-Lasserve and Carole Rakodi (2009)**, "The Limits of Land Titling and Home Ownership," _Environment and Urbanization_ 21(2): 443–462. The most authoritative empirical critique.
- ★★★ **Klaus Deininger (2003)**, _Land Policies for Growth and Poverty Reduction_, World Bank. The flagship World Bank doctrine. Essential for setting up Chapter 3.
- ★★ **Klaus Deininger and Hans Binswanger (1999)**, "The Evolution of the World Bank's Land Policy: Principles, Experience and Future Challenges," _World Bank Research Observer_ 14(2): 247–276.
- ★★ **Tania Murray Li (2014)**, _Land's End: Capitalist Relations on an Indigenous Frontier_, Duke University Press.
- ★★ **Tania Murray Li (2007)**, _The Will to Improve: Governmentality, Development, and the Practice of Politics_, Duke University Press.
- ★★ **Tania Murray Li (2010)**, "Indigeneity, Capitalism, and the Management of Dispossession," _Current Anthropology_ 51(3): 385–414.
- ★★ **Saturnino M. Borras Jr. and Jennifer C. Franco (2012)**, "Global Land Grabbing and Trajectories of Agrarian Change: A Preliminary Analysis," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 12(1): 34–59.
- ★★ **Saturnino Borras and Jennifer Franco (2010)**, "Contemporary Discourses and Contestations Around Pro-Poor Land Policies and Land Governance," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 10(1): 1–32. Land tenure security is central to the dominant development policy narrative and practice. Front and centre in 'land tenure security' frameworks is the security of capitalist investors — a strong critical frame. [PubMed Central](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8567742/)
- ★ **Kevin E. Davis and Michael Trebilcock (2008)**, "The Relationship between Law and Development: Optimists versus Skeptics," _American Journal of Comparative Law_ 56: 895–946.
- ★ **Edésio Fernandes (2011)**, _Regularization of Informal Settlements in Latin America_, Lincoln Institute of Land Policy. For the COFOPRI Peru case study — important comparative reference.
- ★ **Erica Field (2003)**, "Entitled to Work: Urban Tenure Security and Labor Supply in Peru," _Quarterly Journal of Economics_. Pro-titling empirical work.
- ★ **Erica Field (2007)**, "Entitled to Work: Urban Property Rights and Labor Supply in Peru," _Quarterly Journal of Economics_ 122(4): 1561–1602.
- ★ **Edward Glaeser (2011)** on de Soto debate — useful for synthesis.

#### Important debate-shaping critical pieces

- ★★ **Brenna Bhandar (2018)**, _Colonial Lives of Property: Law, Land, and Racial Regimes of Ownership_, Duke University Press. The most theoretically sophisticated recent critique of property formalisation; argues racial regimes of ownership are central.
- ★★ **Ananya Roy (2005)**, "Urban Informality: Toward an Epistemology of Planning," _Journal of the American Planning Association_ 71(2): 147–158.
- ★★ **Anthony Hall and James Midgley (2004)**, _Social Policy for Development_. For the welfare-oriented critique.
- ★ **The Oakland Institute reports** on World Bank land policy, including the response to Deininger. The World Bank clearly follows an ideological blueprint, which focuses on privatizing land and promoting large-scale, capital-intensive agriculture — useful for activist critique tradition. [Oaklandinstitute](https://www.oaklandinstitute.org/our-take/our-land-our-business/response-klaus-deiningers-blog-post-world-banks-land-and-poverty)

### §2.3 History of Land Records in India

This is the longest and most argumentatively dense section. The move: today's Indian land records system is a _palimpsest_ — pre-colonial revenue records overlaid with colonial property records overlaid with post-independence reform records — and DILRMP is trying to flatten this without acknowledging its layered nature.

#### §2.3.1 Pre-colonial record-keeping

**Mughal land revenue and records:**

- ★★★ **Irfan Habib (1999, revised edn.)**, _The Agrarian System of Mughal India 1556–1707_, Oxford University Press. The canonical work. Especially the chapters on revenue assessment and the _zabt_ system. Habib documents how the Mughal _patwari_ and _qanungo_ maintained sophisticated village and pargana-level records well before British arrival.
- ★★★ **Irfan Habib (1982)**, _An Atlas of the Mughal Empire_, Oxford University Press.
- ★★ **Shireen Moosvi (1987)**, _The Economy of the Mughal Empire c. 1595: A Statistical Study_, Oxford University Press. For quantitative depth.
- ★★ **Tapan Raychaudhuri and Irfan Habib (eds.) (1982)**, _The Cambridge Economic History of India, Vol. 1: c. 1200 – c. 1750_, Cambridge University Press. Use chapters 1, 7, 8, 9.
- ★★ **Sumit Guha (1995)**, "An Indian Penal Régime: Maharashtra in the Eighteenth Century," _Past & Present_ 147: 101–126. For Maratha-period revenue practices — directly relevant to Odisha given Maratha rule.
- ★★ **Sumit Guha (2013)**, _Beyond Caste: Identity and Power in South Asia, Past and Present_, Brill. For pre-colonial state forms.
- ★ **Abu'l Fazl** (1590s), _Ain-i-Akbari_, English translation by H. Blochmann and H.S. Jarrett. The primary source on the Mughal bandobast — accessible in standard libraries.
- ★ **Burton Stein (1980)**, _Peasant State and Society in Medieval South India_, Oxford University Press. For the _Vijayanagara_ and earlier South Indian record-keeping.
- ★ **Noboru Karashima (2001)**, _History and Society in South India: The Cholas to Vijayanagar_, Oxford University Press.
- ★ **Hiroshi Fukazawa (1991)**, _The Medieval Deccan: Peasants, Social Systems and States, Sixteenth to Eighteenth Centuries_, Oxford University Press.

**The point to drive home:** The Mughal _zabt-dahsala_ system established by Todar Mal in 1580 was a sophisticated revenue-assessment system, but it was a _fiscal_ system, not a _property rights_ system in the modern sense. For each pargana, the cultivated area of each crop, the yield in maunds per bigha, and the prevailing market prices over the years from 1570–71 to 1579–80 were averaged. From the resulting average yield, the state demanded one-third in kind, valued at the ten-year average price, and converted into a cash rate per bigha that was then entered into the dastur-ul-amal. This distinction is crucial — your section needs to make it visible. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahsala_system)

#### §2.3.2 Colonial settlements and land administration

**On Permanent Settlement:**

- ★★★ **Ranajit Guha (1963/1996)**, _A Rule of Property for Bengal: An Essay on the Idea of Permanent Settlement_, Duke University Press (or Orient Blackswan India edition). The intellectual history of how the Permanent Settlement was designed. Guha shows it was driven by physiocratic European ideas, not Indian conditions: British administrators such as Lord Cornwallis and Philip Francis were far more considerably influenced by the French physiocrats than by Indian conditions on the ground. Read fully — the argument matters for your whole framing. [AbeBooks](https://www.abebooks.co.uk/9788178244822/Rule-Property-Bengal-Essay-Idea-8178244829/plp)

### §2.4 Legal and Institutional Infrastructure for Land Records in India

The argumentative move: India's land records system is constitutionally fragmented (land is a State subject), legally bifurcated (presumptive titling, not conclusive), and institutionally dispersed across four parallel ministries — making the DILRMP integration ambition radical and difficult.

#### Constitutional and statutory framework

- ★★★ **Namita Wahi** (especially the _Seminar_ 2013 piece and the CPR papers cited above).
- ★★ **Shubhankar Dam (2014)**, _Presidential Legislation in India: The Law and Practice of Ordinances_, Cambridge University Press — for ordinance-making affecting land.
- ★★ **N.C. Saxena (2004)**, _Updating Land Records: Is Computerisation Sufficient?_ IDS-LSE Working Paper. The early diagnostic statement.
- ★★ **Carnegie India / Carnegie Endowment** (2019), _Making Land Titles in India Marketable: Using Title Insurance as a Viable Alternative to Conclusive Titling_. For rural areas, the Indian central government initiated the National Land Records Modernization Programme in 2008 with the "ultimate goal of ushering in the system of conclusive titles with title guarantee in the country," as per the Torrens system of titling. Progress under the DILRMP has, however, been slow, and as this paper highlights, many deficiencies still remain. [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://carnegieendowment.org/posts/2019/04/making-land-titles-in-india-marketable-using-title-insurance-as-a-viable-alternative-to-conclusive-titling?lang=en)

#### On presumptive vs. conclusive titling and the Torrens system

- ★★★ **Government of India / NITI Aayog (2020)**, _Model Bill on Conclusive Land Titling, 2020_. Read directly.
- ★★★ **Land Titling Bill, 2011** (Model). Read with the Wire and Legally India critiques — they have important things to say about the inadequacy of the legal architecture.
- ★★ **Benito Arruñada (2003)**, "Property Titling and Conveyancing," in _Research Handbook on the Economics of Property Law_. The Torrens scholarship.
- ★★ **Anirudh Burman (2019)**, "Improving Land Records in India," NIPFP Working Paper. Burman is one of the most prolific Indian scholars on this.
- ★★ **Anirudh Burman and Suyash Rai (2018–2022)**, several NIPFP papers on land records, registration, and titling. Track these on the NIPFP website.
- ★★ **Diya Uday (IGIDR)**, _Maharashtra DILRMP State Report_ (the IGIDR study mentioned earlier). The state reports were presented by Ms Diya Uday, IGIDR, Mr Anirudh Burman, NIPFP and Prerna Prabhakar, NCAER. [NCAER](https://ncaer.org/event/enhancing-the-impact-of-government-efforts-to-modernize-indias-land-records/)

### §2.5 Genesis of DILRMP

The argumentative move: DILRMP did not arise from a vacuum — it emerged from the convergence of (a) decades of unresolved post-reform record obsolescence, (b) the World Bank/de Soto global titling consensus of the 1990s–2000s, and (c) India's broader Digital India agenda. Tracing this genealogy sets up Chapter 3.

#### The lineage scheme by scheme

- ★★★ **Department of Land Resources (various years)**, official documents on:
    - Computerisation of Land Records (CLR), 1988-89
    - Strengthening of Revenue Administration and Updating of Land Records (SRA&ULR), 1987-88
    - National Land Records Modernization Programme (NLRMP), 2008
    - DILRMP, 2016 (Central Sector Scheme conversion)
    - 2021–22 to 2025–26 extension
- ★★★ **Solomon Benjamin, R. Bhuvaneswari, P. Rajan, Manjunatha (2007)**, _"Bhoomi: 'E-Governance', Or, an Anti-Politics Machine Necessary to Globalize Bangalore?"_, CASUMM Working Paper. Foundational critical assessment of the _first_ major land records digitisation in India. The Bhoomi program on the digitization of twenty million land records is considered a 'best practice' model of 'e-governance' to be replicated in other parts of India and elsewhere in poor countries. The main findings, at two levels, contrast conventional wisdom. First the digitization of land records led to increased corruption, much more bribes and substantially increased time taken for land transactions. At another level, it facilitated very large players in the land markets to capture vast quantities of land. [Wordpress](https://casumm.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2008/09/bhoomi-e-governance.pdf)

#### DILRMP evaluation literature

- ★★★ **NCAER (2020)**, _Synthesis Report: Pilot Impact Assessment Study of the Digital India Land Records Modernisation Programme (DILRMP)_. The headline finding — while all three states have pursued the computerisation of land records and of the registration process, the emphasis has varied considerably. The computerisation of textual records has received the greatest attention in Himachal Pradesh, while Maharashtra has focused on automating the registration process. But in all three states, the contribution of the DILRMP to these efforts has been rather muted. [NCAER](https://ncaer.org/event/enhancing-the-impact-of-government-efforts-to-modernize-indias-land-records/)
- ★★★ **NIPFP (2020)**, _Rajasthan DILRMP Impact Assessment_, by Anirudh Burman et al.
- ★★★ **NCAER (2020)**, _Himachal Pradesh DILRMP Impact Assessment_, by Prerna Prabhakar et al.
- ★★★ **IGIDR (2020)**, _Maharashtra DILRMP Impact Assessment_, by Diya Uday et al.
- ★★ **Deepak Sanan (2021)**, "Accelerating Progress In Improving Land Records And Services In India?" _Live Law_. Practitioner-academic synthesis.
- ★★ **Centre for Policy Research (2022)**, _Land Rights and the Digital Revolution in India: Potential and Pitfalls_. (Cited earlier.)
- ★★ **Pritam Pratyush (2023)**, "Illegibly Legible: Outcomes of a Land Records Modernisation Programme in South India," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 23(4): 729–754. The most current critical academic assessment.
- ★★ **K. Thara (2022)**, _SVAMITVA: A Socio-Legal Analysis_, CSEP Working Paper 26. Covers the rural property card scheme that is parallel/complementary to DILRMP.
- ★★ **Shrayana Bhattacharya, Vanita Leah Falcao, Raghav Puri (2017)**, _The Public Distribution System and Welfare_ — for the JAM trinity context.

#### The broader Digital India / JAM context

### A practical workflow note

You don't need to read all of these. Here's how to approach this list:

First, do a **two-day mapping exercise** before reading anything. Take this list and tag each entry against your sections (you can literally annotate this document). For each section, identify the 3 readings you absolutely must read and the 5–10 you'll use for citation density. That gives you a 25-paper "core" and a 50-paper "support" set — totally manageable.

Second, **build the bibliography first**. Get all the full citations into your reference manager (Zotero/Mendeley) before you start writing. This sounds clerical but it saves you weeks at the end.

Third, **don't read linearly**. For each section, read your one most important book _first_, write the section's outline based on that, then plug in supporting works as you draft. So Habib first (then Mughal section), then Guha first (then Permanent Settlement section), then Appu first (then post-independence section). This protects you from the trap of reading 30 papers before writing a word.

Fourth, **use Bhattacharya (2018), Wahi/Bhatia (2018), and the NCAER Synthesis Report (2020) as your three "wrapper" texts**. Read them first across your whole chapter — they cover the entire arc and will help you decide which deeper readings to invest time in.

Fifth, **the Odisha-specific work is best done after you've drafted §2.1–2.4 in draft form**. Otherwise you risk getting lost in Odisha details that belong in Chapter 4.

## Chapter 2: Reading Architecture — Version 2 (Agrarian Political Economy Spine)

### The shift from Version 1 to Version 2

Version 1 gave you a strong reading list organised around **land records as instruments of state legibility** — the Scott/Cohn/de Soto axis. Version 2 keeps all of that but adds a second analytical axis: **land records as instruments of capitalist agrarian transition** — the Marx/Byres/Bernstein/Lerche axis.

Why this matters: Without the agrarian political economy spine, your chapter risks reading as administrative-history-of-records. With it, your chapter becomes an argument about _how the state's effort to make land legible is itself a moment in the longer process of capitalist agrarian transition_, and DILRMP is not just a digitisation project but a _contemporary moment of land-as-commodity formation_ under neoliberal globalisation. This is what gives Chapter 2 its analytical relationship to Chapters 3 (political economy of DILRMP), 4 (Odisha's bypass of agrarian transition), and 5–6 (village/parcel-level findings on land ownership, possession, and exclusion).

The single most important conceptual move you need to make is this: **DILRMP and conclusive titling are best understood as a contemporary form of what Marx called "primitive accumulation" — re-imposed through digital legibility rather than through enclosure with hedges and fences.** Once you set up that argument in Chapter 2, every subsequent chapter has its theoretical anchor.

Now let me restructure the reading list so that this analytical thread runs through every section — without removing what was in Version 1.

---

### A new conceptual subsection: §2.1.1 (or expand into §2.2.1)

I would suggest adding a short conceptual subsection — either inside §2.1 ("Land records and their functions") or at the head of §2.2 — titled something like **"Land records, property, and capitalist transition: a conceptual frame."** This is where you do the heavy theoretical lifting that the rest of the chapter rests on.

This subsection's argumentative move: **records are not neutral; they are the legal-administrative infrastructure through which non-capitalist forms of land tenure are converted into capitalist property — and this conversion is never complete, never one-directional, and always politically contested.**

---

### Reorganising the readings: section-by-section additions

Below I list **new readings to add for Version 2**, alongside notes on where the Marx/Byres/agrarian-political-economy material slots into your existing structure. Star ratings as before: ★★★ = read fully, ★★ = read selectively, ★ = use for citation.

---

#### §2.1 Land Records and Their Functions — additions

The agrarian political economy lens to add here: land records do not merely make land legible to the state for taxation (Scott); they also make land _legible to capital_ for purchase, mortgage, speculation, and accumulation. This is the bridge between Scott and Marx that gives your section conceptual depth.

**Marx — primary readings:**

- ★★★ **Karl Marx (1867)**, _Capital, Volume I_, Part VIII: "So-Called Primitive Accumulation," Chapters 26–33. Especially Chapter 27 ("Expropriation of the Agricultural Population from the Land"), Chapter 28 ("Bloody Legislation against the Expropriated"), and Chapter 32 ("Historical Tendency of Capitalist Accumulation"). Free at marxists.org. The single most important theoretical text for Chapter 2. Marx's argument is exactly what you need: the conversion of common, customary, and feudal land-relations into private property-relations is the _founding violence_ of capitalism, and the law is its instrument. The spoliation of the church's property, the fraudulent alienation of the State domains, the robbery of the common lands, the usurpation of feudal and clan property, and its transformation into modern private property under circumstances of reckless terrorism, were just so many idyllic methods of primitive accumulation. They conquered the field for capitalistic agriculture, made the soil part and parcel of capital. The phrase "made the soil part and parcel of capital" is what land records do, in the long historical view. Read this fully, take notes, and let it inform your entire framing. [Pepperdine Policy School](https://publicpolicy.pepperdine.edu/academics/research/faculty-research/intellectual-foundations/marx-engels/marx_part8.htm)
- ★★★ **Karl Marx (1894)**, _Capital, Volume III_, Part VI: "Transformation of Surplus Profit into Ground Rent," Chapters 37–47. Especially Chapter 37 (introduction to the rent question) and Chapter 47 ("Genesis of Capitalist Ground Rent"). Marx's theory of rent is the analytical machinery for understanding why land records _under capitalism_ differ qualitatively from land records under tributary or feudal modes of production. Use selectively — even reading Chapter 47 alone will give you what you need.
- ★ **Karl Marx (1857–58)**, _Grundrisse_, the section on "Pre-Capitalist Economic Formations." Available at marxists.org. Good for the broader civilisational sweep.

**Marx as applied to land — secondary works:**

- ★★★ **David Harvey (2003)**, _The New Imperialism_, Oxford University Press, Chapter 4: "Accumulation by Dispossession." This is the absolutely essential bridge between Marx's 19th-century account and contemporary land grabs/titling. Harvey reframes primitive accumulation as an ongoing, contemporary process: Accumulation by dispossession is a concept presented by the Marxist geographer David Harvey. It defines neoliberal capitalist policies that result in a centralization of wealth and power in the hands of a few by dispossessing the public and private entities of their wealth or land. Read this chapter fully. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accumulation_by_dispossession)
- ★★ **David Harvey (2005)**, _A Brief History of Neoliberalism_, Oxford University Press, especially Chapters 2–3.
- ★★ **David Harvey (2004)**, "The 'New' Imperialism: Accumulation by Dispossession," _Socialist Register_ 40: 63–87. The essay version of the _New Imperialism_ argument — useful and citable.
- ★★ **Massimo De Angelis (2004)**, "Separating the Doing and the Deed: Capital and the Continuous Character of Enclosures," _Historical Materialism_ 12(2): 57–87. Argues primitive accumulation is continuous, not one-off — directly applicable to your DILRMP framing.
- ★★ **Michael Perelman (2000)**, _The Invention of Capitalism: Classical Political Economy and the Secret History of Primitive Accumulation_, Duke University Press. Excellent intellectual history.
- ★★ **Silvia Federici (2004)**, _Caliban and the Witch: Women, the Body, and Primitive Accumulation_, Autonomedia. Important feminist reading of primitive accumulation — pairs well with Bina Agarwal in §2.3.3.
- ★ **Karl Polanyi (1944)**, _The Great Transformation: The Political and Economic Origins of Our Time_, Beacon Press. Chapters 6 ("The Self-Regulating Market and the Fictitious Commodities: Labor, Land, and Money") is essential. Polanyi's argument that land is a "fictitious commodity" is a sharp tool for your analysis: land records are precisely the technology by which the fiction is administratively maintained.
- ★ **Saturnino M. Borras Jr., Jennifer C. Franco and Chunyu Wang (2013)**, "The Challenge of Global Governance of Land Grabbing: Changing International Agricultural Context and Competing Political Views and Strategies," _Globalizations_ 10(1): 161–179. Bridges to contemporary land grab debate.

**Why this matters analytically for §2.1:** Once you've read Marx and Harvey, you can rewrite the four-functions typology in V1 to read more sharply. The functions become: (a) fiscal — tax extraction from the land; (b) juridical — defining property as alienable; (c) economic — making land _circulable_ as commodity and collateral; (d) governmental — incorporating land-holding subjects into welfare and biometric infrastructures. Functions (b) and (c) are the _capitalist functions_ that Marx explains; (a) and (d) are the _state functions_ that Scott and Foucault explain. India's contemporary land records do all four simultaneously. This is the conceptual move.

#### §2.2 Evolution of Land Records and Titling Systems (Global) — additions

Add an explicit historical narrative of the **global enclosure–privatisation arc** as the _long material history_ underlying the cadastral arc you already had.

**§2.2.1 Pre-colonial — additions** Already strong; just add Polanyi (above) for the conceptual frame, and:

- ★ **E.P. Thompson (1991)**, _Customs in Common_, Penguin. Especially the chapter "Custom, Law and Common Right." For the experience of enclosure from below.

**§2.2.2 Colonial — additions** Your colonial section should make clear that British settlements in India were not just administrative reforms but _attempts to install capitalist property relations_. The Marx-Byres lens makes this explicit.

- ★★★ **T.J. Byres (1996)**, _Capitalism from Above and Capitalism from Below: An Essay in Comparative Political Economy_, Macmillan. The foundational comparative work. Read Chapter 1 and the chapter on India fully. Byres argues that capitalist agrarian transition in different countries took different paths — "from above" (Prussian, landlord-led) versus "from below" (American, peasant-led) — and this typology is _directly relevant_ to how you read the Permanent Settlement (an attempted capitalism-from-above that failed) versus Ryotwari (an attempted capitalism-from-below that also failed).
- ★★★ **T.J. Byres (1991)**, "The Agrarian Question and Differing Forms of Capitalist Agrarian Transition: An Essay with Reference to Asia," in _Rural Transformation in Asia_, eds. J. Breman and S. Mundle, Oxford University Press. The seminal essay.
- ★★ **T.J. Byres (1986)**, "The Agrarian Question, Forms of Capitalist Agrarian Transition and the State: An Essay with Reference to Asia," _Social Scientist_ 14(11–12): 3–67. The original journal version of the argument, freely available online — read this if you can't access the book.
- ★★ **Karl Kautsky (1899/1988)**, _The Agrarian Question_, English translation by Pete Burgess, Zwan Publications. The classical text Byres builds on.
- ★★ **V.I. Lenin (1899/1964)**, _The Development of Capitalism in Russia_, in _Collected Works_ Vol. 3. The Prussian/American paths typology comes from here. Allowing for 'infinitely diverse combinations of elements of … capitalist evolution', Lenin (1960 [1899], 33) had analysed two routes to capitalist agriculture, the American Road and the Prussian Road. Widening the scope, Byres showed that historically specific paths of agrarian transition had developed in different countries in Europe and elsewhere. [Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/joac.12031)

**§2.2.3 Post-colonial — additions**

- ★★★ **T.J. Byres (1974)**, "Land Reform, Industrialization and the Marketed Surplus in India: An Essay on the Power of Rural Bias," in _Agrarian Reform and Agrarian Reformism_, ed. D. Lehmann, Faber and Faber. Crucial for understanding why post-independence land reform failed in India.
- ★★ **Henry Bernstein (2010)**, _Class Dynamics of Agrarian Change_, Kumarian Press. The most accessible and analytically powerful contemporary statement of the agrarian political economy framework. Especially the chapters on "The Agrarian Question" and "Capitalism and Agriculture." This is _essential_. activities agrarian capitalism Agrarian Change agri-input agribusiness agricultural commodities Asia and Africa Brazil capitalist agriculture capitalist farming century chapter class dynamics class formation classes of labour colonial commercial capitalism commodification of subsistence — that's the texture of the book. [Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Class_Dynamics_of_Agrarian_Change.html?id=joxWdg7O0tMC)
- ★★ **Henry Bernstein (2006)**, "Is There an Agrarian Question in the 21st Century?" _Canadian Journal of Development Studies_ 27(4): 449–460. The famous statement of the "agrarian question of capital" vs. "agrarian question of labour" distinction.
- ★★ **Henry Bernstein (1996/2007)**, "Agrarian Questions Then and Now," _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 24(1–2): 22–59. The article that inaugurated the contemporary debate.

**§2.2.4 Contemporary and neoliberal — additions** This is where your existing de Soto / World Bank / Tania Li readings now sit _inside_ a Marxian frame. The argumentative move becomes: the de Soto formalisation thesis is not just a technical proposal but a **contemporary instance of accumulation by dispossession**, where digital land records substitute for physical enclosure as the technology of conversion.

- ★★★ **Saturnino M. Borras Jr., Ruth Hall, Ian Scoones, Ben White and Wendy Wolford (2011)**, "Towards a Better Understanding of Global Land Grabbing: An Editorial Introduction," _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 38(2): 209–216. Classic statement of the global land grab literature.
- ★★★ **Saturnino M. Borras Jr. and Jennifer C. Franco (2012)**, "Global Land Grabbing and Trajectories of Agrarian Change: A Preliminary Analysis," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 12(1): 34–59. Already in V1, but elevate to ★★★.
- ★★ **Michael Levien (2018)**, _Dispossession without Development: Land Grabs in Neoliberal India_, Oxford University Press. **Essential.** Levien is an Indian-born sociologist whose book is the single best application of Harvey's "accumulation by dispossession" framework to contemporary India. Read fully. The argument: the post-1991 Indian state has shifted from being a developmentalist land-acquirer (for dams, factories, etc., in the Nehruvian model) to being a _broker for private capital_ — and DILRMP fits squarely within this shift.
- ★★ **Michael Levien (2013)**, "Regimes of Dispossession: From Steel Towns to Special Economic Zones," _Development and Change_ 44(2): 381–407.
- ★★ **Michael Levien (2015)**, "From Primitive Accumulation to Regimes of Dispossession," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 50(22): 146–157. A really sharp essay.
- ★★ **Tania Murray Li (2011)**, "Centering Labor in the Land Grab Debate," _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 38(2): 281–298.
- ★★ **A. Haroon Akram-Lodhi and Cristóbal Kay (eds., 2009)**, _Peasants and Globalization: Political Economy, Rural Transformation and the Agrarian Question_, Routledge. Excellent volume that brings agrarian political economy to bear on globalisation.

#### §2.3 History of Land Records in India — major additions

This is where the agrarian-political-economy spine yields the biggest analytical dividend. The argumentative move: each of the three historical layers (pre-colonial, colonial, post-independence) is best read as _a particular configuration of land, labour, and surplus extraction_ — and the records are the legal-administrative scaffolding of that configuration.

**§2.3.1 Pre-colonial — additions** Add a paragraph framing Mughal-era records _as part of a tributary mode of production_, distinct from the property-records that colonialism would later install. This sharpens Habib.

- ★★★ **Irfan Habib (1995)**, _Essays in Indian History: Towards a Marxist Perception_, Tulika. Especially the essays on the agrarian system and on classes in Mughal India. Habib himself is a Marxist historian, and reading him this way pulls his argument into the right frame for your chapter.
- ★★ **Irfan Habib (1985)**, "Studying a Colonial Economy — Without Perceiving Colonialism," _Modern Asian Studies_ 19(3): 355–381.
- ★★ **D.D. Kosambi (1956)**, _An Introduction to the Study of Indian History_, Popular Prakashan. The pioneering Marxist account of pre-colonial India. Kosambi's framework is broader and looser than Habib's but useful for the longue durée.
- ★★ **R.S. Sharma (1965)**, _Indian Feudalism_, Macmillan. Classic statement of the "Indian feudalism" thesis — useful even if you take a critical position.
- ★ **Harbans Mukhia (1981)**, "Was There Feudalism in Indian History?" _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 8(3): 273–310. The famous critique of the feudalism thesis.

**§2.3.2 Colonial — major additions** Reframe this section so that the Permanent Settlement, Ryotwari, and Mahalwari are read not just as administrative experiments but as **failed attempts to install capitalist property relations through legal-administrative force**. Guha (which you already have) is the intellectual-history side; what you're adding now is the political-economy side.

- ★★★ **T.J. Byres (1986)**, "The Agrarian Question, Forms of Capitalist Agrarian Transition and the State," _Social Scientist_. Already cited, but use directly here. Byres argues that British colonial land policy repeatedly attempted to replicate an "English path" — landlord-led capitalist agriculture — and _failed_ because the underlying class structure and surplus extraction patterns of colonialism prevented genuine transition. In India, before 1947, colonialism sought unsuccessfully to replicate an 'English model' of transition in eastern India; and how, throughout India, colonialism through surplus appropriation and remittances to Britain prevented the creation of the underlying structural conditions necessary for successful agrarian transition. This is your _core argument_ for §2.3.2 — the records are the legal infrastructure of the failed transition. [ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/scientific-contributions/Terence-J-Byres-79782647)
- ★★★ **Jairus Banaji (1977)**, "Capitalist Domination and the Small Peasantry: Deccan Districts in the Late Nineteenth Century," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 12(33–34): 1375–1404. Brilliant on how colonial land law produced specific class relations in the Bombay Deccan.
- ★★★ **Jairus Banaji (2010)**, _Theory as History: Essays on Modes of Production and Exploitation_, Brill. Especially the essays on commodified peasant production and the colonial mode of production debate.
- ★★ **Hamza Alavi (1975)**, "India and the Colonial Mode of Production," _Socialist Register_ 12: 160–197. Classic statement of the "colonial mode of production" thesis — that India was neither feudal nor capitalist but had a specific colonial form.
- ★★ **Amiya Kumar Bagchi (1976)**, "Reflections on Patterns of Regional Growth in India during the Period of British Rule," _Bengal Past and Present_ 95(1): 247–289.
- ★★ **Amiya Kumar Bagchi (1972)**, _Private Investment in India 1900–1939_, Cambridge University Press.
- ★★ **Eric Hobsbawm (1962)**, _The Age of Revolution 1789–1848_, Weidenfeld & Nicolson. For the European context that produced the Permanent Settlement-era political economists. Pairs with Guha.
- ★ **Daniel Thorner (1956)**, _The Agrarian Prospect in India_, University of Bombay. Older but still cited.

**§2.3.3 Post-independence — major additions** This subsection becomes much sharper if framed around **the failure of post-independence agrarian transition** — the central argument of the entire Indian agrarian political economy tradition. The land records story is _the administrative side_ of that failure.

The Mode of Production Debate (essential — your supervisor will expect you to engage):

- ★★★ **Utsa Patnaik (ed., 1990)**, _Agrarian Relations and Accumulation: The "Mode of Production" Debate in India_, Oxford University Press. Already in V1. Read all the introduction and at least the Patnaik, Rudra, and Banaji essays. This volume contains the canonical 1970s debate among Indian Marxists about whether Indian agriculture was capitalist, semi-feudal, or something else. A passionate debate raged for one-and-half decades among the Indian and foreign scholars of broadly Marxist persuasion on mode of production prevalent in Indian agriculture during the 1960s and 1970s. The participants included prominent scholars like Ashok Rudra, Amit Bhaduri, Utsa Patnaik, Jairus Banaji, John Harriss, Daniel Thorner, Nirmal Chandra, Pradhan Prasad, Hamza Alavi and several others. [Anveshi](https://www.anveshi.org.in/broadsheet-on-contemporary-politics/archives/broadsheet-on-contemporary-politics-vol-2-no-1011/is-indian-agriculture-feudal-semi-feudal-or-capitalist/)
- ★★★ **Amit Bhaduri (1973)**, "A Study in Agricultural Backwardness Under Semi-Feudalism," _Economic Journal_ 83(329): 120–137. The single most-cited paper in the semi-feudalism camp. Short — read fully.
- ★★ **Utsa Patnaik (1976)**, "Class Differentiation within the Peasantry: An Approach to Analysis of Indian Agriculture," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 11(39): A82–A101.
- ★★ **Ashok Rudra (1978)**, "Class Relations in Indian Agriculture," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 13(22, 23, 24).
- ★★ **Pradhan Prasad (1973)**, "Production Relations: Achilles' Heel of Indian Planning," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 8(19): 869–872.

**The neoliberal-era argument (most important for your DILRMP framing):**

- ★★★ **Jens Lerche (2013)**, "The Agrarian Question in Neoliberal India: Agrarian Transition Bypassed?" _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 13(3): 382–404. **Essential.** Lerche's argument that India has _bypassed_ the classical agrarian transition under neoliberal globalisation is the single most important contemporary frame for your thesis. There has been no classic agrarian transition: agriculture's share in gross domestic product (GDP) and in employment has declined, but this has not been offset by industrial growth. Instead, people from rural areas mainly get work in the fast-growing construction sector and in services. The expansion of capitalism has also not led to growth in secure employment. This is your link from Chapter 2 to Chapter 3. [LSE Research Online](http://eprints.lse.ac.uk/86967/1/Shah_Conjugated-oppression.pdf)
- ★★★ **Jens Lerche, Alpa Shah and Barbara Harriss-White (2013)**, "Introduction: Agrarian Questions and Left Politics in India," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 13(3): 337–350. The introduction to the special issue. Read fully.
- ★★ **Jens Lerche (2011)**, "Agrarian Crisis and Agrarian Questions in India," _Journal of Agrarian Change_ 11(1): 104–118.
- ★★★ **Henry Bernstein (2010)**, _Class Dynamics of Agrarian Change_, Kumarian Press. Cited above; in this section, focus on the chapters on "Capitalism, Capitalist Agriculture, and Agrarian Change" and "Class Formation in the Countryside."
- ★★ **Barbara Harriss-White and Judith Heyer (eds., 2014)**, _Indian Capitalism in Development_, Routledge. Especially Lerche's chapter on "Regional Patterns of Agrarian Accumulation in India."
- ★★ **Alpa Shah, Jens Lerche, Richard Axelby, Dalel Benbabaali, Brendan Donegan, Jayaseelan Raj, Vikramaditya Thakur (2018)**, _Ground Down by Growth: Tribe, Caste, Class and Inequality in 21st Century India_, Pluto Press / OUP. **Important for Odisha framing** — the volume includes ethnographic chapters from Adivasi and Dalit areas of central and eastern India. The conjugated-oppression frame (caste/tribe/class/gender) is exactly what your village chapters will need.
- ★★ **Alpa Shah and Jens Lerche (2018)**, "Conjugated Oppression under Contemporary Capitalism: Class, Caste, Tribe and Agrarian Change in India," _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 45(5–6): 927–949.
- ★★ **Jonathan Pattenden (2016)**, _Labour, State and Society in Rural India: A Class-relational Approach_, Manchester University Press.
- ★★ **Akram-Lodhi & Kay (2009)**, op cit. — for global comparative.
- ★★ **Madhura Swaminathan and Vikas Rawal (eds.)**, the Foundation for Agrarian Studies (Bengaluru) project on agrarian relations — multiple volumes since 2010s, with detailed village studies that will give your Chapter 5 a methodological model. **Strongly recommend** browsing their _Review of Agrarian Studies_ journal.

**On accumulation by dispossession in India (the bridge to DILRMP):**

- ★★★ **Michael Levien (2018)**, _Dispossession without Development_, op cit. Already cited; here, focus on the introduction and Chapter 1, which set up the analytical frame.
- ★★ **Ramaa Vasudevan (2008)**, "Accumulation by Dispossession in India," _Economic and Political Weekly_ 43(11): 41–46. Short and useful for citation.
- ★★ **Atul Sood (ed., 2012)**, _Poverty Amidst Prosperity: Essays on the Trajectory of Development in Gujarat_, Aakar Books. Useful Indian application.

- ★★★ **Bina Agarwal (1994)**, _A Field of One's Own_. Already in V1. Reframe in V2 as a _Marxist-feminist_ intervention into the agrarian question of women's land rights. Agarwal explicitly engages with Engels on the relation between property, women's status, and class.

#### §2.4 Legal and Institutional Infrastructure — additions

The political-economy reading of this section: India's legal architecture is _consistent with_ the bypassed-transition argument. The legal regime maintains a juridical fiction of land-as-property even as the actual social relations remain non-capitalist or hybrid. The presumptive titling system _requires_ an active state mediator (the patwari, the registrar) precisely because the underlying social relations have not been fully capitalised — and DILRMP's titling ambition is the project of finally completing that capitalisation. This is a powerful argument and one you can develop here.

Add to existing list:

- ★★ **Upendra Baxi (1986)**, _Towards a Sociology of Indian Law_, Satvahan. For a critical legal-realist frame on Indian property law.
- ★★ **Lawrence W. Friedman (1985)**, _A History of American Law_ — useful background on title insurance and Torrens system as comparators.
- ★★ **Kanchan Chopra and S.C. Gulati (2001)**, _Migration, Common Property Resources and Environmental Degradation_, Sage. For the commons/CPR question that gets erased in the legal architecture.

---

#### §2.5 Genesis of DILRMP — major reframing

Your concluding section in V1 was descriptive (when each scheme was launched, why). In V2, frame the _genesis_ itself as the convergence of three currents:

1. The unresolved agrarian transition (Lerche, Bernstein, Byres) — leaving land records chronically out of date;
2. The neoliberal turn in Indian state policy post-1991 (Levien, Harvey, Borras-Franco) — creating demand for land as commodity;
3. The global titling consensus (de Soto, World Bank, Deininger) — providing the policy template.

DILRMP is what you get when these three currents meet. This framing makes Chapter 2 the analytic foundation for everything that follows.

Add for V2:

- ★★★ **Michael Levien (2018)**, _Dispossession without Development_, op cit. Especially the chapters on the neoliberal Indian state and on land as commodity.
- ★★ **Saturnino Borras Jr. (2009)**, "Agrarian Change and Peasant Studies: Changes, Continuities and Challenges — An Introduction," _Journal of Peasant Studies_ 36(1): 5–31.
- ★★ **Atul Sood, Vipul Paikra and others (2014)**, "Special Economic Zones in India: Are They Tools for Industrialization or Real Estate Speculation?" _Economic and Political Weekly_. Useful for the SEZ-DILRMP connection.
- ★★ **Saturnino Borras and Jennifer Franco (2010)**, "From Threat to Opportunity? Problems with the Idea of a 'Code of Conduct' for Land-Grabbing," _Yale Human Rights and Development Law Journal_ 13: 507–523.

---

### Two new mini-themes to weave through the chapter

#### A. The "land question" as both records-question and transition-question

Throughout the chapter, signal that what you are calling "land records" is really _the administrative face of the agrarian question_. This isn't about words — it's about claim. Once you make the connection, the reader sees that DILRMP is not just a file-cabinet upgrade but an attempt to _administratively complete_ an agrarian transition that never economically completed. This is a strong, citable, original argument that your supervisor will appreciate.

#### B. The continuity from primitive accumulation to digital legibility

Across §§2.2.4, 2.3.3, and 2.5, weave the argument that the Marxian story of enclosure → private property → proletarianisation has a contemporary form: customary land → conclusive titling → land-as-collateral → potential dispossession through default, mortgage foreclosure, or fraudulent record manipulation. The _technology_ has changed (from physical fences to digital records), but the _function_ has not. This is exactly Harvey's "accumulation by dispossession" but applied to land-records modernisation specifically — an analytical move that no one has cleanly made in the Indian land records literature, and which could be a contribution of your thesis.

---

### A revised reading order for your six-week schedule

Given Version 2's expanded scope, I'd suggest tweaking the sequence slightly:

**Week 1, Days 1–3:** Marx (_Capital_ I, Part VIII), Harvey (_New Imperialism_, Ch. 4), Polanyi (_Great Transformation_, Ch. 6) — get the conceptual machinery before reading any India-specific material.

**Week 1, Days 4–7:** Scott (_Seeing Like a State_, intro and Ch. 1–2), de Soto (_Mystery of Capital_, Ch. 1–3), Payne et al. (2009). The legibility/titling axis.

**Week 2, Days 1–3:** Byres (1986 _Social Scientist_ essay, then 1996 book Ch. 1), Bernstein (2010 book, key chapters), Lerche (2013).

**Week 2, Days 4–7:** Habib (revenue-system chapter), Guha (_Rule of Property_).

**Week 3:** Cohn, Stokes, Bhattacharya (_Great Agrarian Conquest_), Banaji (1977), Patnaik volume.

**Week 4:** Appu, Wahi, Levien (_Dispossession without Development_).

**Week 5:** DILRMP-specific (NCAER/NIPFP/IGIDR reports, Benjamin et al., Pratyush 2023, Thara 2022 on SVAMITVA).

**Week 6:** Odisha-specific + drafting + revising.

The trick is that _the conceptual reading in Week 1 makes everything else faster_. Once you have Marx-Harvey-Scott-de Soto in your head, you read every Indian study faster because you know what frame you're putting it in.

---

### A short note on what you might _cut_ from V1 in favour of V2

Honestly, very little. The agrarian political economy spine is _additive_ to V1, not a replacement. But two adjustments worth considering:

First, in V1 I had "Christian Lund (2016)" and "Sara Berry (1993)" prominently in §2.1. These remain useful, but they should now sit alongside Marx-Harvey rather than at the top of the section. The Marx/Harvey frame is more fundamental; Lund and Berry are good post-Marxist amendments.

Second, the "global titling consensus" subsection in §2.2.4 should now be explicitly framed as _the contemporary moment of accumulation by dispossession_ rather than as merely a "policy current." The de Soto / Deininger / World Bank readings are still important, but you read them now as objects of critique within the Marxian frame rather than as neutral policy literature.

---

### What I'd offer to do next

You now have V1 (locked) and V2 (this — agrarian political economy spine added). Three further moves I could help with whenever you're ready:

First, a **detailed prose outline of §2.3** (the longest, most demanding section), section by section, paragraph by paragraph, with the argumentative point of each paragraph specified. This is what most often saves a doctoral chapter from sprawling.

Second, an **annotated bibliography of the absolute core 25 readings** — one paragraph each on the argument, the framework, and what _specifically_ you should take from it for your chapter. This is more time-intensive but extraordinarily useful.

Third, a **conceptual diagram** of how the four functions of land records (fiscal, juridical, economic, governmental) intersect with the three historical regimes (pre-colonial tributary, colonial-capitalist-failed, post-independence-redistributive, neoliberal-titling) — making the palimpsest argument visible at a glance.

Tell me which of these would help most. Whichever you pick, you have everything you need to write a chapter that does justice both to the specifically Indian land records literature _and_ to the wider Development Studies tradition. That dual register is what TISS will expect of you.
