### A few writing-craft points specific to Chapter 4

**On voice.** Chapter 4 should sound _more confident_ than Chapters 2 and 3. By the time the reader reaches §4.5, you should be making _your_ arguments, not just synthesising others'. The chapter is your _transition from student of the literature to producer of analysis_. Write in declarative sentences; use "I" or "this thesis" where appropriate; commit to positions.

**On framing.** Every section should open with the _analytical point_ the section makes, not with administrative description. Bad opening: "Odisha was formed in 1936 from parts of three presidencies and 24 princely states." Good opening: "Odisha's contemporary land governance is shaped less by the laws on the statute books than by the four sedimented institutional inheritances — Bengal, Madras, Central Provinces, and the princely Garhjats — that the post-1936 state never fully reconciled. This section traces those inheritances and argues that their unresolved heterogeneity is the structural pre-condition for DILRMP's differential effects across the state today." That opening tells the reader where you are going.

**On the use of primary sources.** When citing Maddox or the 1958 Administration Enquiry Committee or OSATIP or the Niyamgiri judgement, _quote brief specific phrases_ and then _analyse them_. Do not paraphrase loosely. Primary sources do most analytical work when their language is exhibited and parsed.

**On situating your specific fieldwork villages.** Somewhere in §4.6, in two paragraphs, locate your specific fieldwork villages within the analytical structure you have built — _one paragraph per village_. "Village A sits in the formerly Bengal-zamindari coastal belt of [district], where consolidation operations were completed in [year] and DILRMP RoR computerisation in [year]. Village B sits within Schedule V area of [district], where OSATIP protections govern land transfer and FRA implementation began in [year]. These contrasting positions on the palimpsest are what makes their comparison analytically productive." This is the move that turns "village studies" into "comparative case design."

**On length.** A Chapter 4 of about 12,000-14,000 words is typical. §4.1 should be the longest (3000-3500 words — it does the heaviest historical work); §4.4 next longest (2500-3000 — technical-architectural detail); §4.2, §4.3, §4.5, §4.6 each around 1500-2000; §4.7 short, 500-700.

**On the chapter's relationship to fieldwork.** You wrote that you have field analysis. _Do not put the field analysis here._ Chapter 4 sets up the analytical structure within which Chapters 5 and 6 deliver the field evidence. The temptation will be to write field-rich passages here. Resist it. Save the field material for Chapters 5-6. The exception is §4.6 where you describe site selection and methodology — you may foreshadow with one or two illustrative observations, but not data analysis.

---

### A practical sequence for actually writing this chapter

If I were doing this in your shoes, here is the order:

**Week 1.** Read the Cambridge "Computerisation of Land Records in Odisha" chapter, the World Bank LGAF Odisha report, Wahi et al. CPR Scheduled Areas report, and Padel-Das _Out of This Earth_. These four together give you 80% of the secondary material you need. Take notes organised by section of Chapter 4.

**Week 2.** Read the primary statutes (OSATIP, OLR Act 1960, OEA Act 1951, OSS Act 1958, OCH&PF Act 1972, FRA 2006, PESA 1996) and the primary government reports (1958 Administration Enquiry, 2004 Expert Group on Tribal Land Alienation, 2009 State Agrarian Relations, 2010 Saxena Niyamgiri, 2014 Xaxa). Take focused notes on the _specific provisions_ most relevant to your village fieldwork — alienation restrictions, mutation procedures, settlement operations, FRA claim processes.

**Week 3.** Methodology (Srinivas, Jodhka, Béteille, Harriss, PARI Methods Toolbox). Draft §4.6 first because it is the section that most directly anchors what your villages can show.

**Week 4.** Draft the chapter in this order: §4.1 (historical palimpsest) → §4.2 (post-independence reforms) → §4.4 (contemporary digital architecture) → §4.5 (Scheduled Areas and FRA) → §4.3 (post-1991 political economy) → §4.7 (conclusion) → §4.6 expanded → §4.0 introduction (write last). This sequence builds the most demanding analytical material first when you are freshest.

**Week 5.** Revise. Each section's opening paragraph should make the analytical point of the section. Each section's closing paragraph should set up the next. The whole chapter should make a single coherent argument that ends by pointing forward to Chapters 5 and 6.

---

### The deepest piece of advice I can give you

The viva committee's hardest question on Chapter 4 will be: _"why this state, why these villages, what does Odisha specifically reveal that would not be revealed elsewhere?"_ The strongest possible answer to that question is built into the _palimpsest_ argument. Odisha is exceptional in India for the _density_ and _unresolved-ness_ of its tenure heterogeneity. No other state of comparable size combines (a) post-1936 statehood, (b) merger of 24 princely states, (c) inheritance from three different presidencies, (d) one of the largest Scheduled Area regions, (e) the most aggressive contemporary mining-driven dispossession regime, (f) some of the most ambitious state-level cash transfer schemes, (g) a fairly developed Bhulekh portal that nevertheless interfaces with all these complexities. Odisha is not a "case study" in the weak sense; it is an _extreme case_ in the methodological sense — the place where the contradictions of DILRMP and the broader land-records-modernisation political economy are most visible because they are most layered. Your chapter's job is to make that extreme-case quality intellectually credible. If you achieve that, every subsequent chapter has analytical leverage well beyond the two villages.

You have everything you need now to write this chapter. The references are organised; the architecture is laid out; the analytical spine is articulated; the writing sequence is given. When you sit down to write, you should not be searching for material — you should be deciding what to _leave out_. That is the position you want to be in. You are there now.

If at any later point you want me to do a _compact briefing block for Chapter 4_ (paste-ready for a fresh session, like the one you saved for Chapter 2), or a _detailed prose outline of §4.1 paragraph by paragraph_, or a _draft argumentative paragraph in your voice_ as a template — say so. For now, what is in front of you is enough. Read, organise, write.

## CHAPTER 5 — A COMPLETE GUIDE TO WRITING IT

### The deepest truth about Chapter 5

Chapter 5 is the heart of your thesis. Chapters 2 and 3 built the conceptual scaffolding; Chapter 4 grounded that scaffolding in Odisha's specificity. Chapter 5 is where the evidence speaks. Everything before it has been preparation; everything after it (Chapter 6, the parcel-level chapter, and Chapter 7, conclusion) will be inflected by how well you do this one.

Let me state this bluntly because it matters: **the viva will be won or lost in this chapter**. Your committee will probe Chapters 2-3 to test your scholarly competence; they will read Chapter 4 to test your contextual command; but they will _interrogate_ Chapter 5 to test whether you actually know what you are talking about. The strongest possible position to be in is one where the evidence in Chapter 5 is so vivid, so granular, and so politically intelligent that no examiner can dismiss your conceptual claims as ungrounded.

So my guidance for Chapter 5 is going to look different from what I gave you for Chapter 4. Chapter 4 needed _structural argument_. Chapter 5 needs _narrative discipline and ethnographic craft_ — different skills, equally serious.

---

### What Chapter 5 actually is, methodologically

Chapter 5 is a **village-level political-economic ethnography of land records modernisation**. Three terms in that phrase matter, in this order:

_Political-economic_ — it is not a sociology of village life, not a cultural anthropology of caste relations, not a development-economics impact evaluation. It is a study of how political and economic structures shape — and are reproduced through — the operation of land records.

_Ethnography_ — its evidence is qualitative, granular, situated, person-and-place-specific. Households, individuals, transactions, disputes, and events are the unit of analysis.

_Village-level_ — it operates at a scale (one village, or comparison of villages) where you can claim to know the social fabric in full, not in sample.

Chapter 6, which is parcel-level, complements this by going one scale below — into the soil itself, the boundary, the survey number, the mutation file. Chapter 5 is _household-level_ and _village-level_; Chapter 6 is _parcel-level_. Together they constitute your empirical core.

---

### The argumentative spine of Chapter 5

Every doctoral chapter needs one sentence that runs through it. Here is your candidate:

> **In the villages studied, DILRMP's promise of legibility, transparency, and security has produced not a single uniform outcome but a stratified set of outcomes — beneficial for some households, irrelevant for others, harmful for still others — patterned by caste, class, gender, tenure status, location within the village's historical-tenurial palimpsest, and proximity to the local revenue bureaucracy. The chapter argues that these stratified outcomes are not implementation failures but the predictable distributive effects of a technocratic reform that abstracts from the very social structures (of caste, class, customary tenure, and bureaucratic dependence) that determine who can convert legibility into security.**

That sentence — your refinement of it — is what the chapter must demonstrate. Notice what it does. It refuses the binary "DILRMP succeeded / DILRMP failed." It insists on _who_ gained, _who_ lost, _who_ was unaffected, and _why_ — which is the only honest political-economy answer. It also positions your village evidence as supporting an argument that travels beyond the village: about the distributive consequences of technocratic reform in general.

---

### How a political-economy village-studies PhD would actually build Chapter 5

Let me share what I think the best architectural choice is, because I have seen many village-studies chapters and most make the same error: they describe the village exhaustively, then describe DILRMP exhaustively, then describe their findings exhaustively. By the time the reader reaches the analytical payoff, the chapter is exhausted and so is the reader.

Instead, organise the chapter around **types of households interacting with land records**, not around descriptive themes. Here is the architecture I recommend.

#### §5.1 — The village setting (compressed, analytical)

This section should be _short and analytical_, not exhaustive and descriptive. Three or four pages. Cover:

_Location and history._ Place the village within the Chapter 4 palimpsest — was this part of Bengal/Madras/Central Provinces/princely-state Odisha? When was the last settlement operation? Has consolidation happened? Is it within Schedule V? What is the tenurial inheritance specifically?

_Population and social composition._ Caste composition, key sub-castes, presence/absence of Adivasi households, dominant economic activities. Use a clean table.

_Land profile._ Total agricultural area, distribution by holding size, irrigation status, key crops, prevalence of tenancy and sharecropping.

_Administrative geography._ Which RI circle, which Tahsil, distance to the Tahsildar's office, frequency of RI visits, presence/absence of consolidation records, presence/absence of cadastral maps with reliable boundaries.

_One distinctive feature._ Every village has one thing that makes it ethnographically valuable. Identify it here in one paragraph — it could be a recent land conflict, a faction of FRA claimants, a SVAMITVA pilot, a sub-collector's special intervention, anything. Foreground it.

**Critical writing tip.** Do not be tempted to write 15 pages of village description. Your village is a _site of analysis_, not the _object of analysis_. The reader needs to know enough about the village to follow the analytical argument, not enough to write a separate monograph about it.

#### §5.2 — The bureaucratic interface: how land records actually work here

This is where you describe the _operational reality_ of land records administration in your village, as opposed to the official architecture you described in §4.4. Three sub-themes:

_The chain of officials_. Who is the local RI? How often does he visit? Where does the villager actually go for a mutation — to the RI directly, to the Tahsildar's office, to the _amin_ (the village-level surveyor)? What does it cost in time, in money, in negotiation? This is where Akhil Gupta's _Red Tape_ becomes your theoretical companion — you are documenting exactly the kind of "structural violence" of bureaucracy that he theorised at TIRMP-type interface.

_The hybrid paper-digital reality_. Bhulekh is now functional, but every villager still maintains paper RoRs, every mutation still requires physical signatures and trips, every dispute still references the _patta_ in the cupboard. The friction between the digital record (what Bhulekh shows) and the paper-cum-oral record (what the village knows) is your single most analytically generative observation. Document it carefully.

_Brokers and intermediaries_. Who helps villagers navigate the system? _Pleaders_ outside the Tahsildar's office, local political workers, the relatives-in-government-jobs, the literate son in the family. Brokerage is not corruption — it is an _infrastructural feature_ of the system. Document who serves as broker, what they charge (in money, in loyalty, in vote), and how the introduction of digital records has altered (or not altered) brokerage. Bardhan-Mookherjee's clientelism literature and Akhil Gupta's _Red Tape_ are your theoretical anchors.

#### §5.3 — Stratified outcomes by household type

This is the central analytical section, and how you organise it determines the success of the chapter. I strongly recommend a typology of household-types-in-relation-to-land-records. Here is a typology that works:

_Type A: The Asset-Secure Cultivator._ Households with clear, undisputed, properly recorded land of viable size. For these, DILRMP delivers what it promises: faster mutations, easier access to records, smoother PM-Kisan transfers, usable collateral for credit. Document 4-5 such households. These are typically — but not always — dominant-caste households with longer institutional access. The political-economy point: digital legibility _reinforces_ existing security; it does not create it.

_Type B: The Contested Holder._ Households with land they actually cultivate but whose records are contested — inherited, partitioned, encroached, or simply mis-recorded. For these households, Bhulekh becomes a battleground: it freezes errors in place; it makes correction more difficult by requiring legal action against a digital record that has the authority of the state; it gives adversaries (including state land claims) a new tool. Document 4-5 such households. This is where you tell the _legibility-as-vulnerability_ story most powerfully.

_Type C: The Tenant and Sharecropper._ Households who cultivate but do not own. The 1960 Land Reforms Act formally abolished sharecropping but did not abolish it in practice. PM-Kisan eligibility requires _ownership_, so sharecroppers are excluded. KALIA (which has a separate category for sharecroppers) is the test case for what an inclusive scheme looks like. Document 3-4 such households. The political-economy point: DILRMP's data-driven welfare flows reproduce the formal-informal distinction that decades of tenancy reform tried to dissolve.

_Type D: The Landless Household_. Often Dalit, often agricultural labour. DILRMP simply does not see them. PM-Kisan does not reach them. The village commons that they historically depended on for grazing, fuel, MFP are increasingly digitised as "government wasteland" or privatised. Document 3-4 such households. This is the _invisibility_ story — what the digital record does not record is as politically significant as what it does record.

_Type E: The Adivasi Household in Schedule V (if your village is in or near Schedule V)._ OSATIP-protected, FRA-eligible, often holding land informally on what the record may show as forest or government land. The interface between FRA titles, OSATIP restrictions, and Bhulekh's individual-parcel logic produces specific contradictions. Document 3-4 such households. This is the _jurisdictional-incoherence_ story.

_Type F: The Migrant and Absentee Household._ Households where the main earner has migrated for work and land lies fallow or is cultivated by relatives. Digital records freeze the formal owner in place while the actual cultivator is invisible. Document 2-3 such households. This is the _labour-mobility-versus-tenurial-immobility_ story.

**The critical analytical move.** Each household type yields a _different political-economy lesson_. Together they constitute a stratified picture of what DILRMP actually does in a village. The structure of this section should make absolutely clear that no single household-type's experience is "the" experience of DILRMP — and that is itself the analytical point.

#### §5.4 — Specific institutional encounters

This is where you go granular on specific _moments of encounter_ with the land records system. The strongest ethnographic chapters have 5-8 such micro-narratives. Choose your most analytically generative cases. Possibilities:

_The mutation that took three years_. A specific named (or pseudonymised) case. Walk the reader through it. Who applied, when, on what grounds, where it got stuck, what intermediaries were paid, what the digital record showed at each stage. End with the analytical point: what does this case tell us about the operational reality of digitisation?

_The boundary dispute_. Two households, one survey number, a discrepancy between paper and Bhunaksha. Walk through it. Note what each party brings as evidence — Sabik settlement, panchayat memory, recent measurement, family history. Note what the RI's resolution looked like. End with the analytical point.

_The PM-Kisan exclusion_. A household that should be eligible but is not receiving the payment. Walk through the diagnostic chain: was the Aadhaar seeded, was the bank account linked, was the land record updated, did the eKYC happen, did the ULPIN attach? End with the analytical point about how every step is itself a chokepoint.

_The KALIA-vs-PM-Kisan comparison._ The same household's interaction with both schemes shows what an inclusive vs. land-records-dependent welfare design looks like. End with the analytical point about the political design of schemes.

_The FRA title that has no map._ A household holding an FRA pattas but whose claim cannot be located on Bhunaksha because the area was never properly surveyed. The state has issued a title for territory the state cannot itself map. End with the analytical point about the limits of titling.

_The Subhadra Yojana enrolment._ A specific household's experience of the new Odisha women's cash transfer scheme — what was required, what was easy, what wasn't, and how the household actually uses the money. End with the analytical point connecting this to the electoral-political-economy frame from §3.3.

_The Sabik/Hal mismatch._ A specific case where the historical record and the current record diverge — and what that divergence does to a household's claim.

_The encroachment converted to title._ A government land or commons that a household has cultivated for generations — what is happening to it now that Bhulekh assigns a fixed status?

You should have _roughly six_ such micro-narratives. Each should be 600-1000 words. They should be the most vivid writing in the entire thesis.

#### §5.5 — The everyday political economy of welfare: how DBT and land records interact at household level

This is where you bring the §3.3 argument (DBT as electoral instrument) down to household ethnography. The key empirical questions:

_Who receives what?_ For each household type above, document which DBT schemes they receive, which they are excluded from, and why.

_What do they understand the scheme to be?_ Drèze and Khera's work shows that the gap between official scheme architecture and household understanding is enormous. Document this gap. Many households think PM-Kisan is "Modi's gift," not a programme. Many think KALIA is "Naveen's gift" (the long-serving Odisha CM before 2024) — and Subhadra is from the new BJP government. The labharthi varg construction Aiyar describes is _visible_ in your village field notes.

_How does receipt or non-receipt translate into political loyalty?_ This is sensitive territory and you must handle it carefully. Document what you saw without overclaiming a causal story.

_How is the household's experience of the welfare scheme mediated by the land record?_ This is your distinctive contribution. The land record is not visible to most people; but the land record is the _gateway_ between them and the welfare scheme. Mis-recording, non-recording, contested-recording produces welfare exclusion. Document this.

#### §5.6 — The longue durée: changes over the period of fieldwork

If your fieldwork spans more than a year (and good doctoral fieldwork usually does), this subsection becomes very powerful. Document the changes you observed in real time: a new mutation cycle, a SVAMITVA rollout, a Subhadra launch, a Naksha pilot if applicable. The temporal dimension of ethnographic observation is one of its rarest and most defensible features. Use it.

#### §5.7 — Conclusion: what the village evidence establishes

Two pages. Restate the stratified-outcomes argument. Position Chapter 6 — the parcel-level analysis — as the next analytical move that goes one scale deeper. End by suggesting that the patterns documented here are not idiosyncratic to this village but reflect general structural features of technocratic reform under the political-economic conditions Chapter 3 described.

---

### Writing craft — the things that separate good Chapter 5s from great ones

I want to spend some time on this because it matters more than literature lists.

**On voice.** Chapter 5 is the one place in the thesis where you should sound like a person who has been somewhere and seen things, not an analyst quoting other analysts. The ratio of citations to original observation should be _low_ — perhaps one citation per page on average, sometimes none. The chapter's authority comes from your having been there. The ethnographic literature you have read (Srinivas, Béteille, Harriss, Jodhka, Gupta, Mosse) is _background_ — you should _write like them_, not _cite them constantly_.

**On the use of vignettes.** A vignette — a 200-500 word narrative episode — is the most valuable unit of ethnographic writing. It can do analytical work that no abstract sentence can. Open the chapter with a vignette. Open key subsections with vignettes. Use vignettes to ground every theoretical claim. The reader should be able to _see_ the patwari's office, the RI's notebook, the Tahsildar's chamber, the household courtyard where the conversation about PM-Kisan happened.

**On pseudonyms and ethics.** Pseudonymise person and village names. State in §5.1 that you have done so and on what principle. If your supervisor and your TISS IRB have specific guidance, follow it precisely. Note in passing that the families you spoke to consented; do not labour the point.

**On numbers.** A village ethnography needs _some_ numbers — landholding distributions, caste percentages, scheme coverage rates — but should not be drowned in them. A few well-chosen tables in §5.1, scattered specific numbers in subsequent sections, no large statistical apparatus. Save the statistical apparatus for Chapter 6 (parcel-level), where it is more at home.

**On dialect and Oriya words.** Use them. _Patta_, _khatian_, _Sabik_, _Hal_, _amin_, _jamabandi_, _zamindar_, _mutation_ (English in Indian usage), _ekapadia_, _kisam_, _sahi_, _mauza_, _RI_, _Tahsildar_, _jangal_, _jameen_. Italicise on first use; explain in §1.7 glossary; use freely thereafter. The technical vocabulary is what marks your chapter as an _insider account_. A reader who has not done what you have done could not write this.

**On positionality.** Briefly acknowledge it. You are a Development Studies PhD scholar from TISS Mumbai working in Odisha. This shapes what you see, who talks to you, what they tell you. A page on this in §5.1 (or in your methods chapter, §1.x) is necessary. Do not overdo it — positionality reflection should not crowd out the ethnographic material.

**On dramatising bureaucracy.** Akhil Gupta's _Red Tape_ is the methodological model here. Show the boredom, the waiting, the deferral, the file-pushing, the small humiliations, the moments of unexpected kindness. Bureaucracy is a _texture_ — your chapter should convey that texture.

---

### The complete reading list — organised by what each text gives you

#### The ethnographic-craft canon (read for _how to write_, not for content)

- ★★★ **Robert M. Emerson, Rachel I. Fretz and Linda L. Shaw (2011)**, _Writing Ethnographic Field Notes_, 2nd edition, University of Chicago Press. **The single best practical manual.** Read selectively but seriously. Their guidance on fieldnotes-as-data, on writing initial impressions, on managing the gap between observation and inference, will directly shape your prose.
- ★★★ **Clifford Geertz (1973)**, "Thick Description: Toward an Interpretive Theory of Culture," in _The Interpretation of Cultures_, Basic Books. The foundational essay on what ethnographic writing aspires to.
- ★★★ **John Van Maanen (1988/2011)**, _Tales of the Field: On Writing Ethnography_, University of Chicago Press. Distinguishes realist, confessional, and impressionist tales. Useful for thinking about _register_.
- ★★ **Paul Atkinson (2014)**, _Writing Ethnographically_, SAGE. Compact, practical.
- ★★ **Howard S. Becker (2007/2020)**, _Writing for Social Scientists_, University of Chicago Press. The classic on academic prose discipline.

#### The Indian village-studies tradition (already in Chapter 4 list — re-engage here for tone)

- ★★★ **André Béteille (1965/1996)**, _Caste, Class and Power: Changing Patterns of Stratification in a Tanjore Village_, Oxford University Press. **The single best Indian village ethnography to model your prose on.** The way Béteille moves between caste/class structure and named individuals, between general claim and specific incident, is exactly what you want to aim for.
- ★★★ **M.N. Srinivas (1976)**, _The Remembered Village_, Oxford University Press. The unmatched intimate village monograph. Even if you do not write in this register, read it for what village ethnography can sound like.
- ★★★ **John Harriss (1982)**, _Capitalism and Peasant Farming: Agrarian Structure and Ideology in Northern Tamil Nadu_, Oxford University Press. The political-economic village study most analogous to what you are doing.
- ★★★ **Akhil Gupta (2012)**, _Red Tape: Bureaucracy, Structural Violence, and Poverty in India_, Duke University Press. **Re-read for Chapter 5.** Gupta's theorisation of the everyday operations of state bureaucracy will give you both vocabulary and analytical models for your §5.2 and §5.4.
- ★★★ **Surinder S. Jodhka (2012)**, _Village Society_, Orient BlackSwan. Already cited.
- ★★ **Akhil Gupta (1995)**, "Blurred Boundaries: The Discourse of Corruption, the Culture of Politics, and the Imagined State," _American Ethnologist_ 22(2). Foundational essay.
- ★★ **Stuart Corbridge, Glyn Williams, Manoj Srivastava and René Véron (2005)**, _Seeing the State: Governance and Governmentality in India_, Cambridge University Press. **Highly relevant — they did village ethnographic work on state-society interfaces.**
- ★★ **A.R. Vasavi (1999)**, _Harbingers of Rain: Land and Life in South India_, Oxford University Press. A village monograph that reads as both ethnography and political economy.
- ★★ **A.R. Vasavi (2012)**, _Shadow Space: Suicides and the Predicament of Rural India_, Three Essays Collective. For the writing about agrarian distress at field level.
- ★★ **Jonathan Parry (1999)**, _The Lustrous Heaven: Politics and Ritual in a Caste Society_ and _Death in Banaras_ (1994). Models of dense ethnographic writing.
- ★★ **Jonathan Parry, Jan Breman and Karin Kapadia (eds., 1999)**, _The Worlds of Indian Industrial Labour_, SAGE. Methodologically useful for showing how to mix labour, caste, and political economy at field level.
- ★★ **Mekhala Krishnamurthy (2018)**, _Rethinking Rural Markets in India: Polymorphic Mediations_ / various essays on _mandi_ ethnography. For the ethnographic-political-economy register.
- ★★ **Craig Jeffrey (2010)**, _Timepass: Youth, Class, and the Politics of Waiting in India_, Stanford University Press. For how to write about _waiting_ as an analytical category — directly relevant to bureaucratic encounters with land records.

#### The bureaucracy and everyday state literature (essential for §5.2 and §5.4)

- ★★★ **Akhil Gupta (2012)**, _Red Tape_ — cited above; central
- ★★★ **Veena Das and Deborah Poole (eds., 2004)**, _Anthropology in the Margins of the State_, School of American Research Press. Especially Veena Das's "The Signature of the State: The Paradox of Illegibility" — directly applicable to your title-friction observations.
- ★★ **James Scott (1985)**, _Weapons of the Weak: Everyday Forms of Peasant Resistance_, Yale University Press. For how to write about resistance and negotiation at field level.
- ★★ **Michael Herzfeld (1992)**, _The Social Production of Indifference: Exploring the Symbolic Roots of Western Bureaucracy_, University of Chicago Press. Theoretically generative.
- ★★ **David Mosse (2005)**, _Cultivating Development: An Ethnography of Aid Policy and Practice_, Pluto. Already cited.
- ★★ **Anastasia Piliavsky (ed., 2014)**, _Patronage as Politics in South Asia_, Cambridge University Press. Particularly the chapter on the routine workings of patronage in village context.

- ★★ **Anastasia Piliavsky (2014)**, "India's Demotic Democracy and Its 'Depravities' in the Ethnographic Longue Durée," in her edited volume — useful theoretical anchor on the everyday-political-economy reading of village democracy.

---

### How to actually write this chapter in practice

I will assume you have your field notes, your household survey data, your interview transcripts, your photographs, your map sketches, and a substantial command of the empirical material. With that material in hand, here is the writing sequence I recommend.

**Week 1.** Re-read your field notes systematically. Code them by the typology proposed in §5.3 (asset-secure cultivator, contested holder, tenant/sharecropper, landless, Adivasi/Schedule V, migrant). Identify 5-7 specific cases that will become your micro-narratives for §5.4. Identify the one or two _opening vignettes_ that will introduce the chapter.

**Week 2.** Draft §5.1 (village setting) and §5.2 (bureaucratic interface). These are the _infrastructural_ sections that establish the analytical stage. They should be written carefully but not at maximum effort — the maximum effort is in §5.3 and §5.4.

**Week 3.** Draft §5.3 (stratified outcomes by household type). Allow this to be the longest single drafting effort. For each household type, write four pages: 1-2 pages of analytical framing, 2-3 pages of household-level evidence. This section will go through several drafts.

**Week 4.** Draft §5.4 (specific institutional encounters / micro-narratives). Each micro-narrative gets its own writing session. Write them in your best ethnographic register. Resist the temptation to over-analyse — let the stories carry their own weight.

**Week 5.** Draft §5.5 (DBT-land-records household interface) and §5.6 (changes over fieldwork period). Then write §5.7 (conclusion). Finally, write §5.0 (the chapter introduction), which previews the argument and presents the opening vignette.

**Week 6.** Revise. Cut ruthlessly. The first draft will be too long; the best draft will be 35-40% shorter than the first draft. Most chapters benefit from this compression. Eliminate redundancies, kill darlings, tighten transitions, ensure that every paragraph earns its place.

---

### A few writing-craft details specific to your chapter

**On the opening vignette.** It should be one specific scene, one specific person (pseudonymised), one specific interaction with the land record. It should run 400-600 words. It should _embody_ the chapter's argument without stating it. Then the second paragraph of §5.0 should articulate the argument explicitly.

Example structure for an opening vignette: "[Pseudonym] is seventy-three. We are sitting in the courtyard of his three-room house in [Pseudo-village]. He has placed three documents on the cot between us. The first is a yellowed _patta_ from the 1962 settlement, listing 1.2 acres in his father's name. The second is a printout from Bhulekh, dated November 2023, showing 0.94 acres in his name. The third is a rejection slip from the PM-Kisan portal, dated last month. He looks at me. 'My grandfather paid the _zamindar_. My father paid the government. I have paid the _amin_. And now the computer says my land is less than my father's land. Where did the rest go?'"

That vignette tells your reader within five sentences what the chapter is about: temporality, accumulated tenurial layers, paper-digital-bureaucratic interface, welfare exclusion, and the textured experience of legibility-as-loss. You can never reach this level of efficient evocation through analytical prose alone.

**On voice authority.** When you make a claim, ground it in specific cases. Do not write: "Most landless households in the village did not receive PM-Kisan." Write: "Of the 47 landless households in [village], none received PM-Kisan during the period of fieldwork. Of these, 31 were SC, 9 ST, and 7 backward-caste. None had access to the formal land records system except as agricultural labourers for owners who did." Specificity is authority.

**On theory.** Cite Gupta, Aiyar, Sircar, Drèze-Khera, Wahi, Pratyush, Benjamin, Levien — but cite _briefly_ and _only where they sharpen the field observation_. Do not lecture. The reader is here for your village evidence; theoretical work should illuminate, not dominate. One sentence of theory for every four paragraphs of evidence is roughly the right ratio.

**On the gendered dimension.** Even if it is not the main argumentative thread, ensure that female-headed households, widows whose names should appear in mutation records but do not, daughters who are not inheriting under formal arrangements, and women's interaction with the Subhadra scheme are visible throughout. Bina Agarwal's work is essential for analytical framing.

**On comparisons across schemes.** Where a single household interacts with PM-Kisan, KALIA, Subhadra, MGNREGA, PDS, and the _Forest Rights Act_, document _all_ the interactions. The comparative ethnography of welfare schemes within a household is methodologically distinctive — the same person experiences six different state interfaces, each with different inclusion-exclusion logics, and the cumulative effect is the household's actual relationship with the state.

---

### A final piece of advice — the deepest one

Chapter 5 is where your thesis stops being a thesis-about-DILRMP and becomes a thesis-about-India-as-it-is-now. The best village ethnographies — Béteille's, Srinivas's, Harriss's, Vasavi's, Gupta's, Krishnamurthy's — all have one thing in common: they treat their specific village as a place where general truths about Indian society become visible. They are not parochial; they are _not_ exhaustive descriptions of one place. They use one place to _think with_ about something larger.

Your chapter should do the same. Your village is not just your village; it is a place where the contradictions of post-1991 Indian political economy, of the technocratic turn in welfare delivery, of the colonial palimpsest of tenure, of the electoral political economy of cash transfers, of the gendered structure of property — all of these become visible because you sat in courtyards and listened. The chapter's job is to make those large things visible _through_ the small. That is what ethnography does at its best, and it is what your committee will recognise when you do it well.

You have the material. You have done the work. The reading list above gives you the methodological and theoretical anchors. The architecture I have proposed gives you the structure. What remains is the writing — and the writing of Chapter 5 should be the most enjoyable, most rewarding, and most distinctively _yours_ writing of the entire thesis. The earlier chapters were synthesis; this one is yours.

When you are ready for it, I can prepare a similar guide for Chapter 6 (the parcel-level analysis), a compact briefing block for Chapters 4 and 5 (paste-ready for fresh sessions), or a draft of an opening vignette for §5.0 in your voice as a stylistic template. Tell me when. For now, you have what you need.

## CHAPTER 6 — A COMPLETE GUIDE TO WRITING IT

### What Chapter 6 actually is — and why it might be the most important chapter in your thesis

Chapter 5 was the village. Chapter 6 is the **parcel**. This shift in scale matters more than it might first appear.

Most theses on land records modernisation stop at the village level. They tell you about administrative encounters, household experiences, welfare exclusions, broker networks — all of which Chapter 5 covers. What very few theses do is go one scale below: into the survey number, the _khata_, the _plot_, the boundary itself. They do not measure parcels in the field, compare them against the Bhulekh database, cross-check against Bhunaksha maps, examine the actual chain of mutations that brought a parcel to its current state, or document the _material discrepancies_ between the digital record, the paper record, the cadastral map, the registered deed, and the ground reality.

That is what Chapter 6 does. And it is precisely this move that makes your thesis distinctive.

When your committee asks the killer question — _"why should anyone care that you spent three years on this rather than read NCAER's evaluation reports?"_ — the answer lives in Chapter 6. The NCAER reports work at the _village_, _block_, and _state_ level using statistical aggregation. They cannot examine specific parcels. You can. Your parcel-level analysis is what statistical evaluation can never produce: granular, situated, materially-grounded evidence of how the digital land records system _actually corresponds (or fails to correspond) to the land itself_.

This is the chapter where your thesis stops being one of many critical assessments of DILRMP and becomes the first study to assess it _parcel by parcel, with ground truth_. State that explicitly in §6.0 — your committee needs to see you understand the methodological signature you have built.

---

### The argumentative spine of Chapter 6

> **At the parcel level, DILRMP's promise of accurate, transparent, conclusive land records is contradicted by systematic material discrepancies — between digital RoR and paper RoR, between RoR and Bhunaksha, between Bhunaksha and ground measurement, between Sabik (pre-settlement) and Hal (current) records, between recorded boundary and physical boundary, between recorded extent and measured extent, between recorded kissam (land classification) and actual land use, and between formal owner-of-record and actual cultivator. The chapter argues that these discrepancies are not random errors but structured patterns — distributed unevenly across the village's parcels, with particular regularity at the edges of socially marginalised holdings, contested boundaries, customary tenure, and historical settlement-era inaccuracies. The political-economic significance is that DILRMP digitises and authorises these inherited discrepancies, giving them new official force, with material consequences for cultivation, welfare entitlement, credit access, and dispute risk.**

Your version of this sentence is the chapter's gravitational centre. The argument is that **digitisation of inaccurate records produces authoritative inaccuracy**. This is a sharp, defensible, original claim. It is also exactly what the proponents of DILRMP would resist hearing, which makes it doctorally productive.

---

### How a political-economy scholar with cadastral and village-studies expertise would build Chapter 6

Let me share the architectural logic before getting to sections, because this chapter requires a very specific design discipline.

The chapter is built around a single **comparative-empirical move**: for every parcel you have studied, you compare _what the digital record says_ against _what other sources of evidence reveal_. Those other sources include: (a) the paper RoR/khatian; (b) the Bhunaksha map; (c) ground measurement (yours or the _amin_'s); (d) the registered deed (if available); (e) the household's stated possession; (f) the village's collective memory of the parcel; (g) the Sabik settlement record; (h) the FRA pattas where applicable; (i) the mutation chain (the sequence of recorded transactions).

For each parcel, some of these sources will agree and some will disagree. The _pattern of agreement-and-disagreement is your evidence_. When you aggregate across parcels — and this is critical — you discover that disagreements cluster systematically: by caste of holder, by holding size, by location within the village (e.g. near the commons, near the forest boundary, in the lower-lying _kharif_ land), by historical event (consolidation, settlement re-survey, partition), by tenure type (private, government, forest, common, _abadi_).

So Chapter 6 is essentially a _forensic exercise_ — and you must write it that way. Each section establishes a comparative move; each move yields a class of discrepancy; each class of discrepancy points to a political-economic conclusion.

---

### Section-by-section guide

#### §6.1 — The methodology and ethics of parcel-level work

Open the chapter with this section because the parcel-level method is itself one of your major contributions. Without justifying it, the reader will not appreciate what follows. Keep it tight — three to four pages.

_The sample of parcels._ How many parcels did you study at the granular level? Why these and not others? A purposive sample is fine: parcels stratified by holder caste, holding size, tenure type, location in village. Justify the stratification.

_The cross-checking protocol._ For each parcel, what sources of evidence did you assemble? This becomes a clean methodological checklist. Bhulekh RoR (digital); paper RoR provided by household; Sabik settlement reference; Bhunaksha map screenshot; ground measurement (using GPS, chain-tape, or _amin_ assistance); registered deeds where available; mutation files from the Tahsildar's office; FRA pattas where applicable; oral testimony from the holder; cross-testimony from neighbours.

_The matching matrix._ For each parcel, a comparative table — what each source says about ownership, extent (area), boundary, kissam, mutation history. This matrix is your analytical backbone. Present its structure here; populate it with examples in later sections.

_Ethics._ You have measured land that people consider theirs. Some of the discrepancies your work reveals could be politically explosive — could provoke disputes, could be used against your interlocutors. State plainly what protocols you followed: pseudonymisation, withholding of identifying details, sharing of findings back with respondents, declining to participate in any administrative or legal action based on your data.

_Limits of the method._ Acknowledge them. You cannot survey every parcel. Your GPS is not survey-grade. The _amin_'s chain measurement is itself approximate. You cannot read original Sabik records in colonial-era Oriya script without help. State these limits — they strengthen rather than weaken your authority.

This section establishes the credibility of everything that follows. Get it right.

#### §6.2 — The Sabik–Hal palimpsest at parcel scale

Begin with the deepest temporal layer. Every parcel in your village carries within it a record-history that extends back through Hal settlement (the current settlement, usually 1980s-1990s for most Odisha villages), Sabik settlement (often Maddox-era — 1900s-1920s), pre-Maddox revenue arrangements, and possibly pre-colonial customary memory.

_The comparison._ For 20-25 of your parcels (or however many you have analysed at this depth), document what the Sabik record showed and what the Hal record showed. You will find discrepancies — names changed, extents adjusted, kissam reclassified, boundaries shifted. These are not corrections; they are _layered authoritative statements_, each made at a different historical moment with different methods, different observers, and different administrative purposes.

_The political-economic analysis._ Who gained and lost in the Sabik-Hal transition? This is one of your sharpest analytical moves. In Odisha specifically, where consolidation was incomplete and where the Hal settlement coincided with the start of post-Estate-Abolition allocations to former tenants, the Sabik-Hal comparison reveals whose names were entered, whose were dropped, whose extents grew, whose shrank. This is the historical underbelly of digitisation: Bhulekh digitises Hal records, treating them as authoritative, but Hal records were themselves the product of contested administrative work two generations ago.

_The micro-narrative._ End the section with one or two specific parcels where the Sabik-Hal discrepancy is most analytically generative. Walk the reader through it. Show what Sabik says, what Hal says, what Bhulekh now says, what the family says. Let the data speak.

#### §6.3 — RoR–Bhunaksha disagreement: the textual-spatial divergence

This is the most technically distinctive section, because most thesis writers cannot do it. You can.

The Bhulekh system separates _textual_ land records (the RoR — owner, extent, kissam, encumbrances) from _spatial_ records (the Bhunaksha — the actual cadastral map showing boundaries, neighbours, dimensions). In a properly integrated system, these should agree absolutely: the extent on the RoR should equal the polygon area on the Bhunaksha, the neighbours listed on the RoR should match the adjacent polygons on the Bhunaksha, the kissam classification should match the map's land-use coding. In practice, they do not. They were maintained by different offices, on different timelines, with different standards.

_The comparison._ For each parcel in your sample, compare RoR-stated extent against Bhunaksha-calculated polygon area. Compare RoR-listed neighbours against Bhunaksha-shown neighbours. Build a table of disagreements.

_The patterns._ You will find that disagreements cluster — often at the edges of holdings (where village boundaries meet government land, or near the _baori_, or where forest meets revenue land), often around recently mutated parcels, often where consolidation was incomplete. Document the pattern.

_The political-economic analysis._ When RoR and Bhunaksha disagree, which is authoritative for what purposes? For PM-Kisan eligibility, the RoR is used. For physical demarcation in a boundary dispute, the Bhunaksha is referenced. For ULPIN assignment, both must agree — and where they do not, ULPIN assignment stalls. The chapter shows that this textual-spatial divergence is _productive of new disputes and exclusions_. The literature on this — the Cyberswift, JETIR, and Springer GIS papers I found — frames it as technical errors to be eliminated; you frame it as _political-economic friction_ that produces consequences.

This section deserves a sub-section on the recent attempt to resolve this through ULPIN/Bhu-Aadhaar. ULPIN's promise is precisely to integrate textual and spatial records under a single unique identifier — but where the underlying records disagree, ULPIN assignment becomes itself a moment of dispute resolution, often performed bureaucratically without notifying the affected parties. Document this.

#### §6.4 — Digital record vs paper record vs ground reality: the three-way comparison

This is where you turn to the **physical land itself**. Walk parcels with the household; measure with whatever tools you have; compare against what Bhulekh shows and what the household's paper RoR shows.

_The categories of discrepancy._ You will find: (a) extent discrepancies — recorded extent does not match measured extent (the parcel is smaller or larger than the record says); (b) boundary discrepancies — the recorded boundary lines diverge from the physical boundaries (often hedges, _aals_, bunds, tree lines that have moved with cultivation over decades); (c) location discrepancies — in extreme cases, the parcel itself cannot be located on the map at the stated coordinates; (d) inclusion discrepancies — parts of the cultivated area are not in the record; (e) exclusion discrepancies — parts of the record do not exist on the ground (the parcel has been encroached, lost to road-widening, lost to erosion).

_The patterns by holder type._ Across your sample, document which holders experience which discrepancies. Smaller holders, women holders, Adivasi holders, marginal-caste holders — what discrepancies do they face? What about dominant-caste, larger holders? This is your most important analytical move in this section: you show that discrepancy is _socially distributed_.

_The DILRMP claim and your counter-evidence._ DoLR's official narrative (and the 2022 _Best Practices_ booklet) treats RoR-Bhunaksha-ground harmonisation as the _outcome_ of DILRMP. Your evidence shows it is far from achieved at parcel level. State this carefully — you are not claiming DILRMP has failed; you are claiming that the _level of completion at parcel scale_ is significantly below the level at which it can produce conclusive title security or smooth welfare delivery.

#### §6.5 — Mutation chains: the temporal architecture of records

This is the most narratively rich section. For each of 8-12 carefully chosen parcels, trace the full mutation chain across decades. Inheritance, partition, gift, sale, pre-emption, family settlement, court order, government acquisition, FRA conversion — each is a recorded (or unrecorded) transaction that has shaped where the parcel sits today.

_Where mutations are stuck._ Document the parcels where a mutation is pending — sometimes for years. Document the reasons. Document who is harmed by the delay (often the heir; often the woman who is supposed to be co-recorded; often the buyer in an unregistered transfer).

_Where mutations were never recorded._ Document the parcels where actual cultivation has shifted but the record has not. These are the _invisible transactions_ of the village. Akhil Gupta's _Red Tape_ and Veena Das's _Signature of the State_ are your theoretical anchors here.

_Where mutations were retrospectively recorded._ The most contentious cases. Someone has produced a deed, an oral arrangement, a panchayat memory, and has had a mutation recorded that the original holder disputes. Now Bhulekh shows the contested version as authoritative. The digital record has fixed in place a contested outcome.

_The political-economic frame._ Mutation is the _moment of transfer of legibility_. It is also therefore the moment of greatest contestation — and the moment where bureaucratic discretion is highest. Connect this to your §5.2 discussion of brokerage. Mutation requests pass through the _amin_, the RI, the Tahsildar; at every stage, bureaucratic discretion and brokered access shape the outcome. The digital interface has not eliminated this; it has reorganised it.

#### §6.6 — Kissam, common land, and customary tenure at parcel level

This is the section where your Schedule V villages, if you have them, deliver their most distinctive evidence. Even if your villages are not in Schedule V, the _kissam_ classification system (the categorisation of land into types like _bahal_, _berna_, _anabadi_, _gochar_, _jangal_, _anabadi-jogya-anabadi_, etc.) is itself a critical site of political-economic operation.

_What is and is not classified as private agricultural land._ Bhulekh records private agricultural parcels meticulously. It records government land less well. It records common land — _gochar_ (grazing), _baori_ (water tank), _jangal_ (forest), _padaar_ (uplands) — in ways that are systematically vulnerable to misrecording, misclassification, and conversion. Document specific cases of common-land parcels in your villages where the _kissam_ has been altered, or where common land has been recorded as government revenue land (the precondition for future allocation).

_Customary forest and Adivasi tenure._ Where your villages contain FRA-eligible land, document how it appears (or fails to appear) in Bhulekh. The FRA gives heritable rights but does not always produce a clean entry in Bhulekh. Sometimes a _pattas_ is issued but no Bhunaksha polygon exists. Sometimes the polygon exists but the _kissam_ shows it as forest. The juridical incoherence — heritable individual rights over land that the cadastral system records as forest — is a major political-economic finding.

_The political-economic point._ Customary, common, and forest tenure systems are _structurally illegible_ to a parcel-based digital system. This is not a failure of DILRMP; it is a feature of cadastral modernity. Cite Scott and Cohn (already in your bibliography) and Tania Li's _Land's End_. The conceptual frame links back to Chapter 2.

#### §6.7 — The parcel-welfare nexus: when discrepancy translates into exclusion

This section closes the analytical loop with §3.3 and §5.5. The parcel-level discrepancies you have documented have _welfare consequences_. Show them.

_PM-Kisan exclusions traced to parcel-level problems._ For each PM-Kisan-excluded household you identified in Chapter 5, return to the parcel record and trace the cause. The eKYC failed because Aadhaar was not seeded — _because the mutation record had two slightly different spellings of the same name_. The bank account did not link — _because the RoR extent on Bhulekh did not match the registered deed extent_. The land was deemed "not in your name" — _because of a Sabik-Hal discrepancy that the Bhulekh system has not reconciled_. Document these chains in detail. They are the most compelling evidence that parcel-level inaccuracy translates into welfare exclusion.

_KALIA vs PM-Kisan comparison._ KALIA, the Odisha scheme, was deliberately less land-records-dependent than PM-Kisan. Document the comparative experience of the same household. This evidence directly supports your §3.3 substitution thesis: scheme design choices interact with land record reality to produce different distributive outcomes.

_Credit access._ Loans from cooperatives, regional rural banks, and microfinance lenders require land collateral. Parcel-level inaccuracies — extent discrepancies, missing mutations, contested boundaries — translate directly into denied or reduced credit. Document this where you have evidence.

#### §6.8 — Conclusion: from village to system

Two to three pages. Summarise the typology of discrepancies, the patterns of distribution, the welfare consequences. Then make the move that justifies the whole chapter:

The parcel-level evidence shows that DILRMP has not failed; it has _succeeded at digitising and authorising an inherited regime of inaccuracy_. The political-economic significance is that the inaccuracy is now harder to challenge, because it carries the authority of digital infrastructure. The contest over land records, which was once a contest between people and _patwaris_, has become a contest between people and databases. This shift is not neutral; it disadvantages those who have least access to the means of challenge — to brokers, to lawyers, to Tahsildar appointments, to digital interfaces. The chapter therefore extends the Chapter 3 argument that technocratic reform redistributes rather than eliminates political contestation — and demonstrates that redistribution at parcel scale.

The conclusion should point forward to Chapter 7: what implications does the parcel-level reality have for the conceptual claims of Chapter 2, the political-economic claims of Chapter 3, and the village-scale claims of Chapter 5? What would a different design of land records modernisation look like, given what you have found?

---

### On craft — the specific writing discipline this chapter requires

Chapter 6 cannot afford to sound dry, even though its material is technical. The technical material must be presented with such clarity and consequence that the reader feels the political stakes throughout. Here is how:

**Use the language of forensics.** Words like "evidence," "trace," "establish," "corroborate," "contradict," "reconstruct," "ground-truth," "discrepancy," "match," "divergence" — these belong in this chapter. They give it the texture of methodical investigation rather than ethnographic narrative.

**Use tables and figures sparingly but powerfully.** A clean table comparing RoR-Bhunaksha-measurement for a single parcel can do enormous analytical work. A small figure showing the village parcel-by-parcel discrepancy distribution can replace pages of prose. But do not over-load. Three or four well-chosen tables and two or three figures across the chapter is enough.

**Use parcel identifiers consistently.** Adopt a pseudonymisation scheme that lets you refer back to the same parcel across sections — e.g. "Parcel V1-K12-P03" (village 1, khatian 12, plot 03), preserving the structural logic of the system while protecting privacy. Once you have introduced the scheme, the reader can follow specific parcels through the chapter and see their full forensic profile.

**Hold the line on political-economic interpretation.** The temptation in technical chapters is to drift into administrative description. Resist it. Every category of discrepancy must lead to a political-economic interpretation. Why does this matter? Who gains, who loses, how does this connect to the larger argument? Make these connections explicit, in every section.

**Use the ethnographic voice sparingly but powerfully.** Save your most vivid vignettes for the moments when a parcel-level finding becomes a human story — a widow who cannot mutate her late husband's land because of an extent discrepancy, an Adivasi family whose FRA _pattas_ is for land that does not exist on Bhunaksha. These moments connect Chapter 6 to Chapter 5's ethnographic register and prevent the chapter from feeling abstracted from the people whose lives it documents.

**Show your work.** Where you have measured a parcel, say with what tool. Where you have read a Sabik record, say with whose help. Where you have a screenshot, present it. Where you have a chain-tape measurement, present it. The reader's confidence in the chapter grows in proportion to your transparency.

---

### The complete reading list — organised by what each text gives you

#### The cadastral, GIS, and parcel-level technical literature

- ★★★ **DoLR (Government of India)**, _Digital India Land Records Modernization Programme: Approach/Methodology for Ground Truthing in Resurvey and Map Publishing_. **Primary source.** Lays out the official five-layered approach to ground truthing: HRSI/ORI base layer, grid making, ground truthing of boundaries by surveyor using mobile applications and GNSS rovers, digitization of village boundaries with attributes, parcel/village boundary map published after QAQC and approval. **You must cite this directly** — it is the official methodology against which your evidence is implicitly comparing itself.
- ★★★ **Cadastral Resurvey and Integration of Geospatial Technique using High-Resolution Satellite Image (HRSI): A Case Study in Indian District (2022)**, _Applied Geomatics_ (Springer). Useful for methodological precedent — the paper compares chain-tape survey, computer-aided cadastral digitisation, and HRSI-based methods, and finds significant discrepancies between methods. Cite as comparative methodological grounding.
- ★★★ **Constructing a Seamless Digital Cadastral Database using Colonial Cadastral Maps and VHR Imagery — An Indian Perspective (2015)**, Taylor & Francis. **Essential.** The paper directly addresses the analytical problem you are documenting: paper-based cadastral maps from pre-1920s and 1950s, when digitised, propagate error such that the digitised parcel area is not equal to the authorised area (i.e. true area). Therefore, it is one of the major problems to minimise the effects of such errors to ensure the precision of the area attribute in the GIS database. This paper gives you a _peer-reviewed technical grounding_ for your discrepancy findings. [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/stream/koneru-ranga-rao-committee-on-land-issues-of-the-poor/2021-niti-ayog-report-committee-on-state-agrarian-relations-unfinished-task-in-land-reforms_djvu.txt)
- ★★ **GIS-based Land Information System using Cadastral Model: Case Study of Tirat and Chalbalpur Rural Region of Raniganj in Barddhaman District (2016)**, _Modeling Earth Systems and Environment_ (Springer). Bengal-Bardhaman field study — methodologically applicable.
- ★★ **B.O. Binns and P.F. Dale (1995)**, _Cadastral Surveys and Records of Rights in Land_, FAO. The foundational international reference on cadastral systems.
- ★★ **Peter Dale and John McLaughlin (2000)**, _Land Administration_, Oxford University Press. The standard reference on land administration globally.
- ★★ **Daniel Páez and Jenny Bradley (2015)** and other comparative cadastral literature — useful international anchors

#### The DILRMP technical implementation literature (re-engaged for parcel focus)

- ★★★ **DoLR, _DILRMP Operational Manual and Component-Specific Guidelines_**, especially on cadastral resurvey, mutation procedures, Bhunaksha integration, and ULPIN assignment. Primary, **cite directly** to set the official benchmark against which your parcel evidence is being read.
- ★★★ **DoLR (2022), _Best Practices in DILRMP_ booklet**, already in your Chapter 3 bibliography. Re-read for parcel-level claims. Note specifically the state-by-state claims about RoR-Bhunaksha integration completion — these are the claims your evidence implicitly tests.
- ★★★ **DoLR MIS Dashboard (dilrmp.gov.in)**, primary, for component-wise completion data at state and district level. **Cite Odisha-specific numbers directly**, including ULPIN assignment status, cadastral map digitisation status, RoR-Bhunaksha integration status.
- ★★★ **NCAER (2020), _DILRMP Synthesis Report_** — already cited. Re-read for the methodological precedent and especially for what they could _not_ find using state-level statistics, which your parcel-level evidence fills in.
- ★★★ **NCAER Himachal Pradesh, NIPFP Rajasthan, IGIDR Maharashtra impact assessments (2020)** — already cited. Their methodologies are state-level; your method extends below them.
- ★★ **Anirudh Burman (2019, 2020)**, NIPFP papers on land record integration — already cited
- ★★ **DoLR (2024) NAKSHA programme documentation** — primary, for the urban-cadastral context

#### The literature on land disputes and litigation (essential for §6.5)

- ★★★ **PRS Legislative Research / Daksh / Vidhi Centre for Legal Policy** working papers on land disputes in India. Daksh's land litigation tracking and Vidhi's work on the burden of land litigation are essential. About 25% of all cases decided by the Supreme Court are centered around land disputes. Again, 66% of all civil cases in India are related to land disputes — useful general framing. [Comptroller and Auditor General of India](https://cag.gov.in/en/audit-report/details/25974)
- ★★★ **CPR Land Rights Initiative**, Wahi et al. (cited in Chapter 3) — already cited. Their litigation database is methodologically relevant.
- ★★★ **Land Conflict Watch** (TISS-affiliated, already cited) — for empirical anchoring of dispute typology

- ★★ **Annelise Riles (2006)**, _Documents: Artifacts of Modern Knowledge_, University of Michigan Press. Useful framing for documents-as-objects of analysis.

### How to actually write this chapter

I will assume you have your parcel-level field data, your Bhulekh/Bhunaksha screenshots, your measurement records, your mutation file copies, and possibly some preliminary tables. Here is the writing sequence I recommend.

**Week 1.** Build the parcel-database first. For each parcel in your sample (let us say 40-80 parcels), create a clean comparative table: Bhulekh extent, Bhunaksha extent, paper RoR extent, measured extent, listed owner, actual cultivator, kissam, neighbours, mutation status, FRA status (if applicable), associated welfare scheme outcomes. This database is your analytical infrastructure. Without it built clean, the chapter cannot be written.

**Week 2.** From the database, identify _patterns_ of discrepancy. Cluster parcels by type of discrepancy, by holder characteristics, by location in the village. The patterns will dictate the section structure. Then draft §6.1 (methodology) and §6.2 (Sabik-Hal). These two sections are infrastructural and can be written cleanly.

**Week 3.** Draft §6.3 (RoR-Bhunaksha divergence) and §6.4 (digital-paper-ground comparison). These are the most technically demanding sections. Use specific parcels throughout. Write tables as you write prose; let each table earn its place by making the prose around it more economical.

**Week 4.** Draft §6.5 (mutation chains). This section will be the narrative high point. Each mutation chain you trace should read as a small forensic case study. Take time.

**Week 5.** Draft §6.6 (kissam, commons, customary tenure) and §6.7 (welfare consequences). These sections close the loop with Chapters 3 and 5. Finally, write §6.8 (conclusion) and then §6.0 (introduction). The introduction should preview the methodological signature and the central finding.

**Week 6.** Revise. Cut. Tighten. Check that every section has an opening analytical sentence and a closing political-economic point. Check that figures and tables earn their space. Check that pseudonymisation is consistent. Have a colleague read for clarity — technical writing is hard for readers who do not share your tacit familiarity with the material.

---

### A few specific writing-craft details for this chapter

**On the title.** Consider naming the chapter something more analytically distinctive than "Parcel-Level Findings." Try: "The Parcel Beneath the Portal: Discrepancy, Authority, and the Digital Cadastre" or "What Bhulekh Cannot See: A Forensic Reading of Land Records at Parcel Scale." Titles like this signal the chapter's distinctive contribution.

**On the opening of §6.0.** Open with a single specific parcel. Walk the reader through what Bhulekh says about it, what Bhunaksha shows for it, what the household holds in paper, what you measured on the ground, what the mutation file shows, and what it adds up to. Then in the second paragraph articulate what the chapter does: this parcel is not unique; it is one of N parcels you examined this way; the patterns you found are systematic; and they have political-economic significance that the chapter will demonstrate.

**On illustrations.** Where the data supports it, include: (a) a screenshot from Bhulekh of a single parcel's RoR (anonymised), (b) the corresponding Bhunaksha polygon, (c) a sketch of your ground measurement, (d) a comparative table. These four objects together do enormous evidential work. Use them.

**On numbers and statistics.** Use them sparingly but specifically. "Of the 67 parcels we measured, 22 showed extent discrepancies of more than 10% relative to the Bhulekh-recorded extent. Of those 22, 14 were held by households below 1 hectare; 9 were held by SC or ST households; 16 were held by households that had experienced a mutation in the last decade." This kind of specificity is the chapter's evidential bone.

**On voice authority.** This is the chapter where you can sound _most_ like a person who knows. Not "according to the literature, cadastral records in India often suffer from inaccuracies"; instead, "in our village, 22 of 67 surveyed parcels carried extent discrepancies exceeding 10%; the discrepancies were not random." The difference is everything.

**On overclaiming and underclaiming.** The greatest risk in this chapter is overclaiming on the basis of one village's evidence. Underclaiming is also a risk — being so cautious that the analytical point disappears. The right balance: be specific about what your evidence shows; be careful about generalisation; be confident about pattern-identification within your sample; let the reader extrapolate based on the cumulative weight of your specific findings.

---

### A final piece of advice — the deepest one

Chapter 6 is the chapter where your committee will say either _"this is original work that contributes to the field"_ or _"this is competent but not distinctive."_ The line between the two runs through what you do with the parcel-level material.

If you write Chapter 6 as a careful, granular, forensically disciplined demonstration that digital land records do not match the land they purport to record — and that the patterns of mismatch are politically structured — then you have produced original empirical scholarship. There is no peer-reviewed study at this level of granularity for Indian land records. Your thesis will become a reference work.

If you write Chapter 6 as a series of impressionistic observations about discrepancies, without the systematic comparative architecture, the parcel-level discipline, and the political-economic interpretation, then the chapter becomes another extended case study and the originality drops.

The architecture I have outlined above is what makes the chapter distinctive. The reading list anchors it methodologically. The writing-craft guidance helps you produce prose worthy of the evidence. What remains is the work.

This chapter is yours in a way that no chapter before it could be. Chapters 2 and 3 synthesised existing literature. Chapter 4 set up the Odisha context. Chapter 5 documented village-level experience. Chapter 6 is where you have measured what no one else has measured and tracked what no one else has tracked. The evidence is uniquely yours; the interpretation must be too.

When you are ready, I can prepare any of the following: a compact briefing block for Chapter 6 (paste-ready for fresh sessions); a draft of the opening vignette for §6.0 in your voice; a sample comparative parcel table that you can model your data presentation on; or detailed advice on figures and visualisations for the chapter. For now, what is in front of you is enough. The forensic discipline is the chapter; the chapter is the heart of your thesis. Go and write it.
