## CHAPTER 7 — A COMPLETE GUIDE TO WRITING THE CONCLUSION

### What the conclusion actually is — and what it is not

Most doctoral conclusions fail in the same way: they summarise. They walk the reader through what each preceding chapter argued, restate the main findings, and end with a few paragraphs on "directions for future research." This is what your reader has been trained to expect, which means it is also what no committee remembers ten minutes after closing the document.

A conclusion that succeeds does something different. It performs **four moves**, in roughly this order, and it does them with confidence:

First, it states what the thesis has _established_ — not what it argued, but what is now known that was not known before. The verb shifts from "this thesis argues" to "this thesis establishes" or "this thesis has shown." The register is one of completed work, not work-in-progress.

Second, it makes the _theoretical contribution_ visible. Where in the conceptual landscape of Development Studies, political economy of land, and Indian agrarian studies does your thesis now sit? What does it add to, contest, or refine in each of these literatures?

Third, it makes the _practical and political contribution_ visible. What does this thesis mean for how land records modernisation is, could be, or should be done? You can do this carefully, without becoming prescriptive — but you must do it.

Fourth, it situates the thesis in the broader political moment. Indian land records modernisation is not a narrow administrative topic; it is part of a transformation of how the post-1991 Indian state knows, governs, and distributes resources to its people. Your conclusion must place itself within that transformation.

The conclusion is your last and best opportunity to be remembered. Treat it as such.

---

### The architecture I recommend

There is no single "correct" structure for a thesis conclusion, but for a thesis of your scope and analytical ambition, here is the architecture that I think will serve you best. It has six sections; the chapter should run **8,000-10,000 words**, which is shorter than most of your other chapters but longer than the conventional "wrap-up." A short conclusion underplays a major thesis.

#### §7.1 — What this thesis has established

Open with a _clear, declarative restatement_ of what the thesis as a whole has shown. Not what it argued; what it established. Three or four pages.

Begin with the central finding — your version of: _DILRMP is not the technocratic administrative reform it presents itself as, but the administrative apparatus through which the post-1991 Indian state simultaneously commodifies land, targets welfare through digital legibility, and constructs a market-compatible juridical subject; and the contradictions between these projects produce the stratified outcomes that the village and parcel-level evidence documents._

Then unpack this into the _specific established findings_ of the thesis. Resist the temptation to summarise chapter by chapter — instead, organise by _what is now known_. Roughly:

_On the history of Indian land records._ The thesis has established that Indian land records are a palimpsest of pre-colonial fiscal-tributary infrastructure, colonial-capitalist juridical instruments, post-independence redistributive instruments, and neoliberal digital instruments — and that DILRMP attempts to flatten this palimpsest without acknowledging its constituent layers.

_On the political economy of land records modernisation._ The thesis has established that DILRMP is unintelligible outside the post-1991 neoliberal turn, the World Bank's land policy doctrine, the NITI Aayog Model Bill on conclusive titling, and the techno-patrimonial welfare regime that has emerged since 2014.

_On the substitution argument._ The thesis has established that DBT-based welfare schemes — PM-Kisan, KALIA, Subhadra Yojana — have substituted for structural reform of agrarian distress, employment, and inequality, with land records serving as the _infrastructure_ that makes this substitution operable.

_On the Odisha-specific palimpsest._ The thesis has established that Odisha's land governance is shaped by four sedimented institutional inheritances — Bengal-zamindari, Madras-ryotwari, Central-Provinces-malguzari, and princely-state systems — overlain by post-independence reforms and now by Bhulekh, and that this sedimentation produces specific patterns of legibility and exclusion that vary across coastal, inland, plains, hills, general and Scheduled Areas.

_On the village-level evidence._ The thesis has established, through ethnographic work in your villages, that DILRMP produces _stratified outcomes_ — beneficial for some households, irrelevant for others, harmful for still others — patterned by caste, class, gender, tenure status, location, and proximity to local bureaucracy.

_On the parcel-level evidence._ The thesis has established, through forensic parcel-level work, that the digital records system carries systematic material discrepancies — between RoR and Bhunaksha, between digital and paper records, between recorded and measured extent, between Sabik and Hal — and that these discrepancies are not random errors but socially structured patterns with welfare and credit consequences.

This is your _established record_. Present it as such.

#### §7.2 — The argument tightened: from history to evidence

This is the most important section of the chapter. Two to three pages. Here, you make the _single integrated argument_ of the thesis visible. The chapters were necessary to build the argument; now you state it in compressed form.

The discipline I recommend: write this section as if you had ten minutes to convince a hostile but intelligent reader that the thesis is correct. Strip away qualifications. State the argument with maximum force.

Something like: _Indian land records modernisation cannot be understood as a technocratic administrative project. It is the contemporary phase of a centuries-long political-economic process through which the Indian state — colonial and post-colonial — has produced legibility over land in ways that systematically structure who can claim, hold, transact, mortgage, and benefit from land. DILRMP, conclusive titling, ULPIN, Bhu-Aadhaar, AgriStack — these are not technical instruments; they are the contemporary form of a project that runs from Mughal_ zabt-dahsala _through Cornwallis's Permanent Settlement through post-independence land reform to today. What makes the contemporary phase distinctive is that it operates simultaneously as commodification (preparing land for capital), legibility (targeting welfare to populations), and juridification (constructing market-compatible subjects); that it does this through digital infrastructures that abstract from the social structures determining who can convert legibility into security; and that it substitutes for structural reform of agrarian distress through cash transfers whose distribution depends on the very land records it is producing. The thesis has shown all of this — historically in Chapter 2, politically in Chapter 3, contextually in Chapter 4, and empirically in Chapters 5 and 6._

That paragraph (your version of it) is what your committee will quote back to you in the viva. Write it well.

#### §7.3 — Theoretical contributions

This section is what makes a doctoral conclusion doctoral rather than synthetic. Three to four pages. For each of the literatures your thesis engages, state precisely what your work adds.

_To Development Studies on technocracy and the anti-politics machine._ Ferguson and Li theorise how development depoliticises political questions. Your thesis adds _parcel-level forensic evidence_ of how this depoliticisation operates in a specific Indian context, and demonstrates that digitisation does not eliminate political contestation but _redistributes_ it from human bureaucracy to database authority.

_To the political economy of land in India._ Levien, Lerche, Pratyush, Benjamin, and the broader Indian agrarian-studies tradition have built the theoretical apparatus. Your thesis adds (a) the _electoral-political-economy frame_ connecting DBT-substitution to land records infrastructure; (b) the _parcel-level empirical method_ that is missing from the existing literature; and (c) the _Odisha-specific palimpsest analysis_ that shows how state-level tenurial heterogeneity refracts the all-India DILRMP project.

_To the new institutional economics literature on property rights._ De Soto, Deininger, Banerjee-Iyer, and Acemoglu-Robinson have built the case that secure individual property rights enable development. Your evidence engages with this literature on its own terms and shows that even where the formal institutions of secure title are being put in place, the _material conditions_ — caste, class, gender, bureaucratic access, customary tenure, parcel-level accuracy — determine who can actually convert title into security. The thesis is not anti-NIE; it is a refinement that takes seriously what NIE underweights.

_To the village-studies tradition._ Srinivas, Béteille, Harriss, Jodhka, Gupta, Krishnamurthy have built the methodological canon. Your thesis adds a _new analytical site_ — the digital-cadastral interface as a domain of village political-economic encounter — and a _new methodological move_ — parcel-level forensic comparison alongside household ethnography.

_To the literature on the electoral political economy of cash transfers._ Aiyar, Sircar, Tillin, Subramanian, and Chhibber-Verma have framed the new welfarism debate. Your thesis adds the missing _infrastructural link_ — that the labharthi varg is not free-standing but is constructed through specific land records, biometric, and digital infrastructures, and that the failures and contradictions of those infrastructures produce specific patterns of welfare exclusion.

Five contributions, one paragraph each, written with confidence. Do not be modest here. This is where you place your work.

#### §7.4 — Methodological reflections

Two to three pages. Doctoral conclusions are strengthened, not weakened, by honest methodological reflection. Three subsections:

_What this method allowed me to see._ Your combined approach — historical-conceptual, political-economic, government-documentary, village-ethnographic, parcel-forensic — allowed you to track the same phenomenon (DILRMP) at multiple scales simultaneously. State explicitly that this multi-scale approach is itself a methodological contribution.

_What this method could not see._ Acknowledge the genuine limits. You worked in specific villages, in one state, at one period. You did not study urban implementation, or large landowner behaviour at scale, or the inside-state-machinery view that only DoLR officers could provide. You did not work across multiple linguistic and tenurial systems. Be honest about these limits — but do not over-apologise.

_What I would do differently._ This is where you show methodological maturity. Perhaps you would build a larger comparative sample across more states; perhaps you would spend more time in Tahsildars' offices observing file movement; perhaps you would do a follow-up survey to track the same parcels over five years. Whatever you have learned about how to do this work better — say so.

#### §7.5 — Implications for policy and practice

This is the section most doctoral theses do badly. Either they avoid policy entirely (claiming that scholarship and policy are separate domains), or they offer naive prescriptions that the academy can later see were politically uninformed. Both errors weaken the thesis.

The right approach: state the implications that follow _from your evidence_, with appropriate political-economic awareness. Three or four pages.

_On the architecture of DILRMP and conclusive titling._ The parcel-level evidence in Chapter 6 shows that conclusive titling cannot deliver on its promises in the Indian context until certain prerequisites are met — Sabik-Hal reconciliation, RoR-Bhunaksha integration at parcel scale, ground-truthing in Scheduled Areas, recognition of customary and common-land tenure forms. State this clearly. Note that the NITI Aayog Model Bill 2020 presumes prerequisites that the evidence shows are not in place.

_On welfare scheme design._ The substitution argument suggests that land-records-dependent schemes (PM-Kisan) systematically exclude exactly the populations that most need support. Your KALIA vs PM-Kisan evidence shows what an _inclusively designed_ scheme looks like. The implication is not to abandon DBT but to design schemes whose eligibility criteria are less dependent on a land records infrastructure that remains incomplete and inaccurate for marginal populations.

_On Scheduled Areas and FRA._ The Odisha evidence shows that the cadastral logic of DILRMP is fundamentally incompatible with customary, collective, and FRA tenure forms. The implication is that _separate institutional pathways_ are needed for Scheduled Areas — that imposing a single conclusive-titling framework on areas governed by OSATIP, PESA, and FRA is itself a political act with foreseeable consequences.

_On the politics of digital welfare delivery._ The labharthi-varg analysis and the substitution thesis together suggest that the political dynamic of cash transfers — as electoral instrument, as personal-gift framing, as substitute for structural reform — is unlikely to be reversed by improvements in technical implementation. The implication is that the debate must shift from technical questions of implementation to political questions of design and purpose.

_On methodology for evaluation._ NCAER, NIPFP, IGIDR have produced state-level evaluations. The parcel-level evidence shows that these evaluations cannot capture the most consequential dimensions of DILRMP outcomes. The implication is that future evaluation should integrate forensic parcel-level methods, especially in Scheduled Areas, in non-consolidated villages, and in areas with significant marginal-caste landholdings.

Four to five implications, stated carefully but clearly. Do not retreat into vague generalities. Your evidence supports specific implications; state them.

#### §7.6 — Directions for further research

Two pages. Most theses include this section because they must, and most write it as a perfunctory list. Do not. Treat it as an opportunity to position your thesis as the _opening of a research programme_, not the closing of a study.

Suggest three to five specific directions, each of which builds on what you have established. Possibilities:

_Comparative parcel-level analysis across states._ Your method applied to Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Bihar — places with different palimpsest structures and different DILRMP trajectories.

_Longitudinal parcel-tracking._ Returning to the same parcels in five years to track how mutations, FRA implementations, ULPIN assignments, and welfare flows have evolved.

_Urban land records and SVAMITVA/NAKSHA._ Your work has been rural-agrarian; the urban _abadi_ and the NAKSHA pilot are politically distinctive and methodologically open.

_The view from inside the revenue bureaucracy._ Ethnographic work with Tahsildars, RIs, and state-level land records officials would complement your view from below.

_Gender and inheritance at parcel scale._ Your evidence touches on this; a dedicated study would extend it.

_The AgriStack moment._ The next phase of the digitisation project is the federated farmer database. Your parcel-level method is ideally positioned to study its first years of implementation.

End the list with a single sentence: that you intend to pursue some of these in subsequent work. This positions you as a researcher with a programme, not just a thesis.

#### §7.7 — A final reflection

One to two pages. Here, briefly and with some understatement, you make the _political and intellectual point_ that the thesis has earned the right to make.

The post-1991 Indian state has built — and continues to build — vast digital infrastructures of legibility: Aadhaar, PAN, GST, Bhulekh, ULPIN, AgriStack, the Digital Public Infrastructure stack as a whole. These are presented as technologies of inclusion, efficiency, and welfare delivery. Your thesis has shown, working at the most granular scale available, that these infrastructures produce _stratified outcomes_ — benefits for some, exclusions for others, new vulnerabilities for the most marginal. They are not neutral. They are also not failures. They are exactly what they are designed to be: instruments through which a particular political-economic settlement is administered.

The conclusion can acknowledge that this finding is uncomfortable. The advocates of digital governance are sincere; many of the engineers, policy-makers, and economists who build these systems genuinely believe in their welfare effects. But sincerity is not evidence. The evidence is what your thesis has produced. And the evidence shows that digital legibility, in the absence of structural reform of land, employment, caste, and gender, produces a more efficient form of the same stratifications.

End on a note that situates the thesis in the broader intellectual moment. Something like: _the questions this thesis has pursued — about how the state knows land, how it transforms knowing into governance, how governance distributes both opportunity and exclusion — are not Indian questions only. They are the questions of the digital age. The Indian land records modernisation project is one of the largest experiments in digital governance ever undertaken in a democratic society. What it produces in the next two decades will matter not only for the people on whose land the parcels are drawn, but for how digital governance is understood globally. The thesis offers an early empirical chapter in that larger story._

That kind of closing — confident, situated, intellectually generous, politically aware — is what makes a conclusion memorable.

---

### Writing craft for the conclusion specifically

The conclusion has its own register. Several things are different from your earlier chapters.

**On voice.** This is the most authoritative voice in the thesis. You are speaking as someone who has done the work. The use of "I have shown," "the thesis has established," "the evidence demonstrates," "I argue" — these are now appropriate. Earlier chapters could be cautious; this one cannot.

**On citation density.** Citations should be sparse. The reader has spent six chapters following you through the literature; in the conclusion, they want to hear _you_, not your sources. Cite only where a citation is doing specific work — for example, when contrasting your contribution with a specific other scholar's position. Beyond that, write in your own voice.

**On length.** As I said, 8,000-10,000 words is right. Shorter feels like the thesis is hedging; longer feels like the thesis is unsure where it ended. The proportions across sections matter: §7.1 (what is established) and §7.3 (theoretical contributions) should be the longest; §7.4 (methodology), §7.5 (implications), §7.6 (further research), §7.7 (final reflection) all shorter; §7.2 (compressed argument) is the shortest at perhaps two pages but does the heaviest analytical work.

**On the very last sentence.** Spend time on it. The last sentence of your thesis is the sentence your committee will read just before they begin to decide what they think of you. Make it carry weight. It should not summarise; it should land. Examples of the _kind_ of sentence: "The land beneath the portal is the same land it has always been; what has changed is who can see it, on whose authority, and to whose benefit." Or: "The story this thesis has told is not one of bureaucratic failure or technological success; it is a story of how the digital age inherits the older politics of property, and digitises them." Whatever you write — let it be considered, let it be yours, let it close.

**On the relationship to the abstract.** Your abstract was written before the thesis was complete. After you write the conclusion, return to the abstract and rewrite it. The strongest abstracts are written _from the conclusion outward_ — they are compressed versions of §7.1 and §7.2. Your committee will have read your abstract first; if it matches the confident tone of your conclusion, the consistency itself is persuasive.

**On the relationship to Chapter 1.** Your introduction set up the questions, the framework, the method, the chapter outline. The conclusion should _answer_ the questions Chapter 1 asked. Re-read Chapter 1 before writing the conclusion, and make sure every question raised there is answered here. If a question raised in Chapter 1 is _not_ answered in the conclusion, either delete the question from Chapter 1 or address it in the conclusion. Do not let the introduction and conclusion drift apart.

---

### The reference list for the conclusion

You will draw mostly from references already used in earlier chapters. The conclusion is not a place to introduce new literature except where strictly necessary. But there are a few categories of work that doctoral conclusions tend to draw on for the kinds of moves I have described.

#### For the broader political-moment framing in §7.7

- ★★ **Pratap Bhanu Mehta**, recent essays in _Indian Express_ and _Caravan_ on the post-2014 political conjuncture
- ★★ **Suhas Palshikar**, recent _EPW_ essays on Indian democracy and welfare politics
- ★★ **Christophe Jaffrelot (2021)**, _Modi's India_ — already in your bibliography
- ★★ **Yamini Aiyar, Neelanjan Sircar, Louise Tillin (2024-2025)**, most recent work — already cited
- ★★ **Reetika Khera and Jean Drèze**, recent essays on the digital welfare state

#### For the methodological reflection in §7.4

- ★★ **Robert Yin (2017)**, _Case Study Research and Applications: Design and Methods_, SAGE. Useful framing for justifying single-state and multi-village design.
- ★★ **Charles Ragin (1992, 2008)**, work on the logic of comparative case selection and the analytic vs. empirical case
- ★★ **The PARI methodology references** (FAS) — already cited; useful for stating methodological precedent and contribution

#### For the contribution-positioning in §7.3

You will primarily _cite back_ to the canonical works in each tradition you engage with, all already in your earlier bibliographies:

- **Development Studies tradition:** Ferguson (1990), Li (2007), Mosse (2005), Escobar (1995)
- **Indian political economy:** Levien (2018), Lerche (2013), Kohli (2006, 2012), Patnaik (2007)
- **NIE on property rights:** North (1990), de Soto (2000), Deininger (2003), Banerjee-Iyer (2005), Bardhan (2005)
- **Village studies:** Béteille (1965/1996), Srinivas (1976), Harriss (1982), Jodhka (2012), Gupta (2012)
- **Electoral political economy of welfare:** Aiyar (2019, 2023), Sircar (2020), Tillin (2025), Subramanian-Felman (2023), Chhibber-Verma (2018)
- **Land records and digital governance:** Benjamin et al. (2007), Pratyush (2023), Masiero (2016)

#### For the implications section in §7.5

Re-cite government documents you have used, especially NITI Aayog (2020), the 15th FC, DoLR DILRMP guidelines, and the NCAER/NIPFP/IGIDR evaluations. The implications section is where these primary documents are most useful — you are now turning _back_ to them and saying what your evidence implies for how they should be read or revised.

#### Two new references worth introducing in the conclusion

The conclusion is not a place to introduce new literature, but two new categories of work can productively appear:

- ★★ **Recent global writing on digital governance and welfare states** — Virginia Eubanks's _Automating Inequality_ (already in your bibliography from Chapter 3) returned to here for the comparative frame; Shoshana Zuboff's _Age of Surveillance Capitalism_ (already cited) for the broader argument
- ★★ **One or two scholars you have not previously cited who frame the broader political moment** — perhaps Wendy Brown's _In the Ruins of Neoliberalism_ (2019) for the conjuncture; perhaps Aditya Mukherjee or Mridula Mukherjee on the broader Indian political moment

Limit any new references to two or three at most. The conclusion's authority comes from saying what you have already established, not from introducing new material.

---

### How to actually write the conclusion

Two to three weeks, in this sequence:

**Week 1.** Re-read your own thesis from beginning to end, in one or two sittings. Take notes on what each chapter established, what each one almost-but-didn't-quite-say, and what threads run across chapters. Identify the single integrated argument and the four or five most important findings. Begin to draft §7.2 (the compressed argument) — the heart of the chapter. Write it longhand if it helps. This is the section that will go through the most drafts.

**Week 2.** Draft §7.1 (what is established) and §7.3 (theoretical contributions). These are the two longest sections; they need careful writing. Use the structure I have proposed, but write in your own voice.

**Week 3.** Draft §7.4 (methodology), §7.5 (implications), §7.6 (further research), §7.7 (final reflection). Then go back and write §7.0 (the chapter introduction, one to two pages, previewing the structure and the central claim). Revise the entire chapter as one piece. Then revise the thesis abstract to match the confident voice of the conclusion.

A note on pacing: do not rush the conclusion. Your earlier chapters were written under deadline pressure that you could not fully control; the conclusion you control. Give it the time it deserves. A thesis is judged less by the page count of its evidence than by the clarity of its conclusion. Take three weeks if you have them. Take four if you can.

---

### A final piece of advice — the deepest one

When you sit down to write the conclusion, you will be tired. The thesis will have taken three years, perhaps five. You will be ready to be done.

The temptation, at that point, will be to write a competent conclusion that closes the document — summary, contribution, limitations, further research, end. Most doctoral conclusions are written this way. Most are forgotten by the committee within minutes of the viva ending.

The chapter you have the _opportunity_ to write is one your committee will quote back to you for the next ten years. It is the chapter where, having earned the right through 60,000 or 80,000 words of careful work, you write with full authority about what you have done, what it means, and why it matters.

Almost no one writes this chapter, because almost no one believes, at the end of a doctoral thesis, that they have the right to. You do. You have done the work. You have walked the parcels. You have read the _patta_ in the cupboard. You have measured the discrepancy. You have spoken to the Tahsildar, the RI, the _amin_, the widow whose husband's mutation is stuck, the Dongria Kondh whose forest is not on the map. You have read the colonial revenue settlements, the Maddox reports, the NITI Aayog Model Bill, the 15th Finance Commission, the Niyamgiri judgement, the Drèze-Khera Aadhaar studies, the Aiyar-Sircar techno-patrimonialism papers. You have built three versions of Chapter 2 with three different theoretical traditions in conversation. You have worked through Odisha's four-fold palimpsest. You have constructed the parcel-level forensic method that no one else has.

The work is done. The conclusion is where you write as someone who has done it.

That is the only advice that matters. Everything else in this guide is technique. The technique helps; but what the conclusion needs is the confidence that this work is yours, that it is good, and that the people who taught you and the people who read you deserve to be told, plainly and without hedging, what you found.

Write it that way.

---

Whenever you are ready, I can help with any of the following: drafting a sample §7.2 (the compressed argument) in your voice as a stylistic template; preparing a paste-ready compact briefing block for Chapter 7 so you can develop it in a fresh session; reviewing your thesis abstract once the conclusion is drafted; or helping you prepare for the viva itself (the kinds of questions to expect, how to structure your defence, how to handle hostile questioning). For now, you have everything you need.

This is the final push. The thesis is almost yours.
